## Page 1


Relative symplectic cohomology in complex
projective spaces
Adi Dickstein∗and Yaniv Ganor
Abstract
Relative symplectic cohomology is an invariant of compact subsets of a closed
symplectic manifold, introduced by Varolgüneş [45]. There are many examples
of computations of this invariant over the Novikov field, but the collection of
computed examples over the Novikov ring is still quite limited. One reason for
this is that such computations require determining the relevant Floer complexes
for Hamiltonians that are not necessarily C2-small Morse functions. In this work,
we present a computation of relative symplectic cohomology over the Novikov
ring for balls and their complements in CP n. Our computation relies on explicit
descriptions of Floer complexes, in the Morse–Bott setting with cascades, for J-
shaped Hamiltonians on CP n. This allows us to deduce new estimates for the
stable displacement energy of the boundaries of balls in CP n.

1
Introduction
1.1
Background
Symplectic topology began as a geometric language for Hamiltonian mechanics, pro-
viding a coordinate free framework for the study of the group of Hamiltonian diffeomor-
phisms. Liouville’s theorem states that these diffeomorphisms are volume preserving,
and hence a natural question rising from this fact is whether there exist finer symplec-
tic invariants. Also, Darboux’s theorem states that every symplectic manifold is locally
isomorphic to an open set in R2n equipped with the standard symplectic form. As a
consequence of this, symplectic manifolds admit no local invariants, hence the need for
global properties, which are the subject of symplectic topology.
In his seminal paper [23] from 1985, Gromov provided an answer to this question
using pseudo-holomorphic curves. He proved his famous non-squeezing theorem which
later led to the theory of symplectic capacities.
3


## Page 4


The next landmark was the development of symplectic homology, pioneered by Floer
and Hofer, in the late 1980s and 1990s, and further developed by many other experts
throughout the years, see [16, 17, 8, 9] for instance. This homology, which is based on
Floer theory, serves as a refinement of the notion of capacity and associates a filtered
module to a given Liouville domain. This homology theory is endowed with algebraic
structures and has functorial properties.
This theory, however, does not obey a general local-to-global principle. In general,
the symplectic homology of a Liouville domain is not reconstructible from that of the
elements of an open cover.
In 2018, Varolgüneş introduced a variant of symplectic homology called relative
symplectic cohomology [45]. Relative symplectic cohomology associates a module
over the Novikov ring Λ≥0 to any compact subset of a closed symplectic manifold,
together with restriction maps for inclusions of compacts. The novelty of this version
is that it admits a local-to-global principle: it satisfies a Mayer–Vietoris property for
commuting subsets, i.e., zero sets of Poisson-commuting functions.
The definition of relative symplectic cohomology is complicated, combining Floer
theory with non-Archimedean algebra; hence, its computation, even in elementary cases,
such as the empty set and the manifold itself, is not an obvious task. In this paper, we
consider basic interesting examples and develop techniques that provide a full descrip-
tion.
The relative symplectic cohomology can be taken with coefficients in the Novikov
field Λ. In this setting, the local-to-global principle becomes extra useful, since in many
examples the relative symplectic cohomology over the field vanishes, and short exact
sequences over a field split. As a result of that, several computations over the Novikov
field have been carried out. See [42, 40, 11, 22, 26, 41, 26] for several prominent results
in the Novikov field setting.
Such a computation requires, first of all, the explicit computation of the Floer com-
plexes associated to Hamiltonians that are not necessarily C2-small, and, in addition,
the computation of all continuation maps between these Floer complexes. To the best
of our knowledge, the only place where an explicit expression of the cochain complex in
such a situation has been displayed is Varolgüneş’s thesis [44], for the case of disks in
the sphere S2.
In contrast, the goal of the present work is to obtain a full computation over the
Novikov ring.
1.2
Main results
The main results of this paper are a full computation of the relative symplectic
cohomology with coefficients in the Novikov ring for toric balls and their complements

## Page 6


We consider the complex projective space CP n equipped with the Fubini–Study
symplectic form, normalized to have Gromov width 1. Throughout the paper,
whenever we mention the “capacity” of a set, we mean the Gromov width of its
interior.
Note that the minimal first Chern number of CP n is n+1. The standard moment
map on CP n is the function Φ: CP n →Rn given by
Φ(z) =

image of Φ is the n-simplex Kn(1). The standard closed toric ball in CP n
of capacity ∆, for some ∆∈(0, 1), is defined as the preimage of the n-simplex
Kn(∆) under Φ, i.e. Φ−1(Kn(∆)).

1.3
Restriction maps
The relative symplectic cohomology is a functor, it assigns a module to a compact
set and a restriction map to an inclusion of compacts. Therefore, it natural to ask for
a computation of the restriction maps. Here we do this for two balls.
Theorem 1.7. Let B∆′ ⊂B∆be standard closed toric balls in CP n with capacities ∆′

1.4
Stable displaceability and computational results
Relative symplectic cohomology with Novikov ring coefficients provides a more pre-
cise characterization of a subset’s symplectic properties. In his thesis [44], Varolgüneş
demonstrates that quantitative information can be extracted from this invariant.
Given a module A over the Novikov ring Λ≥0, its capacity is defined to be
c(A) = inf{r > 0 : T rA = 0}.
Note that c takes values in [0, +∞]. The module A is called torsion if c(A) < +∞.
For example, the capacity of Λ>0 is +∞, in contrast to the capacity of Λ(a,b], for some
a < b, which is b −a.
We caution the reader that the term “capacity” is used in two distinct senses: for
modules, it refers to the capacity defined above, whereas for subsets, as stated earlier,
it refers to the Gromov width of the interior.
In his thesis [44, Remark 4.2.8], Varolgüneş proved that given a closed symplectic
manifold (M, ω) and a compact subset K ⊂M, the displacement energy of K is bounded
from below by the capacity of SH∗
M(K; Λ≥0). In particular, if K is displaceable then
SH∗
M(K; Λ≥0) is torsion, and if c(SH∗
M(K; Λ≥0)) = +∞then K is non-displaceable1.
In addition, Varolgüneş proved that stable displaceability implies that SH∗
M(K; Λ≥0) is
torsion. We complete the picture as follows:
Theorem 1.11. Let (M, ω) be a closed symplectic manifold and K ⊂M be a compact
subset. Then the stable displacement energy of K is bounded from below by the capacity
of SH∗
M(K; Λ≥0).
The following result is an immediate application of Theorem 1.11 and the compu-
tation we made of the relative symplectic cohomology of the complement of a ball in
CP n.

of [13].

Boundaries of Balls—The Use of Mayer–Vietoris
Propositions 1.8 and 1.9 provide a precise formula for the restriction map from the
relative symplectic cohomology of CP n to that of a ball, and to that of the ball’s com-
plement, respectively. Both balls and their complements are preimages of closed subsets
under the standard moment map on CP n. Consequently, these sets commute (see [45,
Theorem 1.3.7] and [11, Remark 1.14]). We can therefore consider the corresponding
Mayer–Vietoris exact triangle for a toric closed ball and the complement of a toric open
ball, provided they form a cover of CP n. Since the relative symplectic cohomology of
these sets vanishes in odd degrees and the restriction maps are known, a purely algebraic
computation yields the relative symplectic cohomology of the intersection. This inter-
section is a shell—specifically, the difference between a closed toric ball and a smaller
13


## Page 14


open toric ball. While we do not carry out the computation in this paper, it is entirely
within reach.
We give a full statement in the case of ball boundaries.

1.5
Review–relative symplectic cohomology
Let (M, ω) be a closed symplectic manifold. The relative symplectic cohomology
is a contravariant functor from the category of compact subsets of M with respect to
inclusion to the category of modules over the Novikov ring. Let us describe how it acts
on objects. For a full definition see Section 8.1. Given a compact subset K ⊂M, the
relative symplectic cohomology of K in M is defined as
SH∗

hat denotes the T-adic completion of Λ≥0-modules (see Section 7.1.1).
The grading of a generator x ∈P◦(Hi) is given by a Conley–Zehnder type index
which is well-defined modulo 2NM, where NM is the minimal first Chern number of
(M, ω).
Remark 1.18. The cohomology SH∗
M(K; Λ≥0) is independent of the specific choice of
Hamiltonians Hi and other auxiliary data. To establish this, and more generally to work
with SHM, it is often advantageous to use an alternative definition based on homotopy
theory; see Section 8.1 and [45] for further details.
1.6
A brief review of the proof of Theorem 1.1
We start with the following acceleration data. Consider the standard moment map
Φ: CP n →Rn, and let B∆denote the toric ball of capacity ∆, whose image under Φ is
the corner simplex of side length ∆. We set Hℓ(z) = hℓ(⟨Φ(z), (1, 1, . . . , 1)⟩) for every
z ∈CP n, where hℓhas a graph as depicted in Figure 1a and Figure 1b below.

Ideally we would like to construct acceleration data by using the Hamiltonians
(Hn)∞
n=0 and choosing homotopies from each Hn to the next, Hn+1, by a monotone
interpolation, (see Section 8.1.2 for a precise definition of the notion of an acceleration
datum), and for an almost complex structure to use Jstd, the standard complex struc-
ture of CP n. However, to achieve regularity we have to perturb this ideal data: We
perturb the almost complex structure Jstd for the definition of the Floer complexes of
Hn, and we perturb the homotopies between them for the definition of the continuation
maps, which enables us to construct a direct system of Floer complexes. See the start
of Section 6 for a discussion on the perturbed data.
In the setting of [45] that we use, the differentials in Floer complexes we use are
weighted by topological energy of the solutions encoded in the exponent of the Novikov
parameter. We show that each matrix element of the differential, ⟨dx, y⟩, is of the form
AT E where A ∈Q, T is the Novikov parameter and E ∈[0, +∞). A similar property
holds for a continuation morphism.
This is due to Equation (11), which shows that in monotone symplectic manifolds,
the energy of Floer and continuation solutions is determined by the index difference of
the 1-periodic orbits. Thus, we may also define Floer complexes and continuation maps
over Z, equivalently, setting T = 1, and we compute these first.
Our method of computing the differential involves two stages. First we show that
most matrix elements vanish, by proving that the corresponding moduli spaces are
empty. Then, we compute the other matrix elements either by identifying them with
the matrix elements of a local computation in Cn, or deduce them by an argument using
the fact that continuation maps are quasi-isomorphisms.
To prove that the moduli spaces are empty, we first prove that they are empty for the
17


## Page 18


unperturbed acceleration data, then show that emptiness persists under small enough
perturbations. See Section A.3.
Our methods require certain symmetries of the Floer data (e.g., a U(n) symme-
try, crucial to our argument), which necessitates the use of autonomous Hamiltonians,
and thus a Floer theory based on cascades.
In this framework, the Hamiltonian is
autonomous, and its 1-periodic orbits form a collection of smooth, finite-dimensional
submanifolds of the loop space. By choosing a Morse function on each manifold, we
define a chain complex generated by their critical points. The differential in this setting
counts “cascades”: trajectories consisting of Floer cylinders interspersed with gradient
segments of the Morse functions, introduced by Frauenfelder, see [19]. See Section 2 for
a review of Morse–Bott theory in Floer theory, using cascades.
For non-degenerate Hamiltonians, the cascades theory coincides with the standard
Floer theory. Consequently, we demonstrate that relative symplectic cohomology may
also be computed using Floer complexes defined via cascades, and therefore we may use
this model for our computations.
Let us now describe the 1-periodic orbits generating CF(Hℓ; Z). The 1-periodic
orbits of Hℓcome in three types of families.
(i) The global minimum at 0,
(ii) ℓspheres S2n−1 consisting of the points where the slope is integral,
(iii) and the global maxima, forming a copy of CP n−1, at the divisor at infinity.
The first family contributes a generator which we denote by ˇx0, each sphere contributes
a pair of generators corresponding to the minimum and maximum of a perfect Morse
function, yielding in total ˇx1, ˆx1, . . . , ˇxℓ, ˆxℓ, where the hats correspond to maxima and
the checks to minima. Lastly, the divisor at infinity contributes generators, which we
denote by ˇxℓ+1, . . . , ˇxℓ+n. The acceleration data and the generators are discussed in
Section 3.2.
We consider two symplectic trivializations of TM along the non-constant generators,
which we use in index computations. The first, denoted τB, is obtained by trivializing
TM over CP n \ D∞, which is symplectomorphic to a ball. The second, denoted by τT
is obtained by identifying CP n \ (D1 ∪. . . ∪Dn ∪D∞) with an open set in T ∗Tn, and
trivializing the tangent bundle there. These trivializations are discussed in Section 3.1.
The trivialization τT has the crucial property that the indices of all the non-constant
generators are either 0 or 2n −1, regardless of winding.
The main tools we use to prove the vanishing of matrix elements are:
Index formula: Given a symplectic trivialization τ of TM along the generators, and
u a flowline with cascades u1, . . . , um, the local dimension of the moduli space at

Intersection formula: If τ is τB or τT, then the first relative Chern number can be
computed in terms of the intersection number with the divisors D∞or D1 ∪. . . ∪
Dn ∪D∞respectively. This is developed in Section 5.1
Positivity of intersections: If the Hamiltonian vector field is tangent to a complex
divisor, then Floer solutions intersect positively with it, see [20, Lemma 4.3].
Moreover when the asymptotes of a Floer solution are fixed points lying on the
divisor, one can define asymptotic intersection numbers, for which, under certain
assumptions, a lower bound can be obtained. This was proven by Seidel in [39,
Eq. (7.22)] and is slightly refined here in Proposition 5.9. With Floer flowlines
with cascades, we encounter a particular type of asymptotic intersections, where
two consecutive cascades are asymptotic, one at ∞and the other at −∞, to a
divisor consisting of fixed points. We show that such intersections are positive in
total, see Lemma 5.11.
We prove that the following matrix elements vanish, see Theorem 5.19:
• ⟨dˆxi, ˆxj⟩= 0 for every 1 ≤i, j ≤ℓ.
• ⟨dˇxi, ˇxj⟩= 0 for every 0 ≤i, j ≤ℓ+ n.
• ⟨dˇxi, ˆxj⟩= 0 for every 0 ≤i ≤ℓ+n and 1 ≤j ≤ℓ, except where i = j and n = 1.
• ⟨dˆxi, ˇxj⟩= 0 for every 1 ≤i ≤ℓand 0 ≤j ≤ℓ+n, except where j ∈{i−1, i+n}.
To demonstrate our methods, we sketch the proof of the fourth item, in the case
1 ≤i, j ≤ℓ.

indices computed in Table 1, from Section 5.18, into the index formula (3), we obtain
(n + 1)u · D∞= n(j + 1 −i),

and
j + 1 −i = k(n + 1).

and bounds on energy and the number of cascades, detailed in Section A.2.

maps in cohomology indirectly. Then using an algebraic lemma, see Proposition 7.23,
and since in our particular case, completion induces a surjective map in homology, see
Claim 9.12, we are able to compute the restriction maps on the homology level using
the limit models.

## Page 25


1.7
Discussion
1.7.1
More computational examples
Expanding the library of explicit computations for the relative symplectic coho-
mology of various closed symplectic manifolds and compact subsets remains a highly
desirable goal. We present here several interesting such cases.
Ellipsoids in CP n
Unlike balls, ellipsoids are not U(n)-invariant, and neither are the critical submani-
folds associated with compatible acceleration data. For this reason, we cannot displace
the 1-periodic orbits from the relevant divisors, as we do in the case of the ball, as
described on Page 18. This leads to a failure of the positivity of intersections; conse-
quently, we cannot establish the obstructions for Floer and continuation trajectories as
we do elsewhere in this paper.
Balls in other toric manifolds
A standard ball in a toric manifold is a symplectic ball that is invariant under the
toric action on the manifold, where the induced action coincides with the standard toric
action on a ball in Cn. Up to the action of SL(n, Z), we can assume that the image of
a standard ball in a toric manifold of dimension 2n is an n-simplex of the form
{(x1, . . . , xn) ∈Rn :
n
X
i=1
xi ≤∆and x1, . . . , xn ≥0}
for some ∆> 0. In this case, there is no obstacle to using the tools we present in
this paper. However, the process of finding obstructions for the Floer and continuation
trajectories is more complicated, as there are more toric divisors, and hence many more
generators with a greater variety of Conley-Zehnder indices.
Neighborhoods of complex hyperplanes
In Claim 3.5, we show that the complement of a point in CP n can be seen as the
line bundle O(1) over D∞= CP n−1. Thus, the complement of a ball in CP n can be
seen as a disk bundle over the divisor D∞, and the complement of this disk bundle, as
a subset of CP n, is just a ball.
This situation is a particular case of the Biran decomposition, see [4]. In CP n with
the standard Fubini-Study metric, it is claimed there that for every divisor D ⊂CP n,
there is an embedding of a disk bundle over D into CP n such that the complement of
25


## Page 26


the image is an isotropic skeleton. In the case of D∞⊂CP n, the disk bundle is O(1)
and the complement is just a point. Another important example is the smooth quadric
hypersurface:
D = {[z0 : · · · : zn] ∈CP n : z2
0 + · · · + z2
n = 0}.
Here, the disk bundle will be O(2) and the skeleton will be a Lagrangian copy of RP n.
In general, we can consider a divisor of the form
D = {[z0 : · · · : zn] ∈CP n : zk
0 + · · · + zk
n = 0},
for some k ∈N. The associated disk bundle will be O(k) and the skeleton will be an
isotropic CW-complex.
It would be highly interesting to find a systematic way to compute the relative
symplectic cohomology of disk bundles over such divisors and of their complements.
Computations using Maurer–Cartan elements
In a recent work [5], Borman–El-Alami–Sheridan use Maurer–Cartan elements to
compute quantum cohomology over a Novikov-type ring, denoted by R in their paper,
for Liouville domains obtained by removing divisors D1, . . . , Dk from closed monotone
symplectic manifolds, under the assumption that
2c1(TM) = λ1 PD(D1) + · · · + λk PD(Dk),
where λ1, . . . , λk ∈(0, 2] ∩Q.
(5)
They model the quantum cohomology as Floer homology of Hamiltonians closely
related to those appearing in acceleration data, and the authors conjecture a connection
between relative symplectic cohomology over the Novikov field of the Liouville skeletons
and deformations of symplectic homology. It would be highly interesting to establish a
connection between their results and relative symplectic cohomology over the Novikov
ring; such a connection would likely lead to many new examples of explicit computations
of this invariant.
Let us mention, though, that these techniques do not seem to be capable of recov-
ering the results of the present paper, since the complement of a ball in CP n is a not
exact and has a concave end rather than being a Liouville domain. Moreover, although
balls in CP n are Liouville domains and can be obtained by removing a copy of CP n−1
from CP n and shrinking along the Liouville flow, Equation (5) above does not hold in
this case, since
2c1(CP n) = 2(n + 1) PD(CP n−1)
and 2(n + 1) > 2.
26


## Page 27


1.7.2
Künneth formula and computations
Given two closed symplectic manifolds M, M ′ and compact subsets K ⊂M and
K′ ⊂M ′, Varolgüneş constructed in [44, Section 4.3] a Künneth morphism
SH∗
M(K; Λ≥0) ⊗SHM′(K′; Λ≥0) →SHM×M′(K × K′; Λ≥0).
In general, this morphism is not an isomorphism, mainly due to the failure of the tensor
product to commute with either homology or the completion. Varolgüneş proved that
for K = M and K′ = M ′, this morphism is indeed an isomorphism.
First of all, it would be very interesting to find exact conditions under which this
morphism is either injective or surjective, or to embed it in some sort of a Künneth short
exact sequence. Second, even if we cannot use a direct combination of the Künneth
morphism and our computations of relative symplectic cohomology, it seems possible
to apply our techniques and compute it directly in simple product cases; for instance,
for polydisks in S2 × S2.
1.7.3
Product structure
Tonkonog–Varolgüneş defined in [42] a product structure on the relative symplectic
cohomology with coefficients in the Novikov field. Later, Dickstein–Ganor–Polterovich–
Zapolsky generalized the product structure to coefficients in an arbitrary algebra over
the Novikov ring using a construction at the chain level; see [11, Sections 2, 4]. Dickstein
provided in his thesis, see [10, Appendix A], more details on this construction, based
on counting perturbed pseudo-holomorphic pairs-of-pants. Also, Abouzaid–Groman–
Varolgüneş developed a much more general construction of a chain level product, using
the language of framed E2 structures; see [1].
We believe that our tools can be used for computing the product structure induced
by the chain-level construction described in [10, 11]. Moreover, using the positivity of
intersections, we might be able to isolate the contribution of pairs-of-pants that do not
intersect the divisor D∞= CP n−1, and thus apply this computation to balls in Cn.
1.7.4
Continuity conjecture
Given ∆∈(0, 1) and a decreasing sequence (∆k)k∈N in (0, 1) that converges to ∆, a
computation using Theorem 1.1 and Theorem 1.7 shows that
lim

could lead to new applications of symplectic rigidity, using techniques similar to those

1.7.6
Categorification
As we know from [45], the relative symplectic cohomology is a functor from the
category of compact subsets of a given closed symplectic manifold to the category of
modules over the Novikov ring. It would be highly interesting to generalize this functor
to the “category of pairs of a closed symplectic manifold and a compact subset, i.e.,
letting the symplectic manifold itself vary as well. The first problem is that this cat-
egory is not yet established. While a natural candidate for morphism is a Lagrangian
correspondence in the product, the composition of such morphisms remains problem-
atic. Nevertheless, using quilted Floer cohomology, see [46], one may try to produce
morphisms between relative symplectic cohomology modules of different manifolds, in-
duced by a Lagrangian correspondence in their product. Currently, it seems that such
questions are mostly open.
29

1.7.7
Torsion in general symplectic manifolds
Varolgüneş conjectured in his thesis [44, Remark 3.3.5] that for every closed sym-
plectic manifold (M, ω) and every compact subset K with non-empty interior (in the
sense of Section 1.4), the capacity of the relative symplectic cohomology of K is positive.
In this section, we confirm this conjecture for CP n.
Indeed, let K ⊂CP n be a compact subset with non-empty interior. We can find a
closed toric ball B such that its image under a symplectomorphism is contained in K.

In a joint work with Frol Zapolsky, currently in preparation, we establish a similar
inequality for any compact subset of an arbitrary closed symplectic manifold. We also
apply this result to establish a new symplectic rigidity phenomenon, specifically the
non-existence of certain symplectic isotopies subject to specific constraints.
Organization of the paper.
In Section 2, we survey the adaptation of Floer theory
to the setting of Morse–Bott with cascades. We start in Section 2.1 with our conventions
3The case of CP 2 is a conclusion from the fact that Ham(CP 2) acts transitively on symplectic balls
in CP 2. See [27].
30


## Page 31


and an overview of the Robbin–Salamon index and classical Floer theory. Section 2.2
presents the Morse–Bott condition for Hamiltonians and an adapted index for 1-periodic
orbits. Sections 2.3 and 2.4 concern the definition of cascades and the construction of
the differential and continuation maps. This construction is generalized in Section 2.6
for induced maps from higher homotopies, using the language of cubes.
Section 3 concerns the geometry of CP n. We discuss the construction of CP n as a
symplectic cut and use it to present radial functions, which constitute the prototype of
the Hamiltonians used in this paper. In Section 3.1, we present three charts of CP n that
will be extremely helpful for computing Robbin–Salamon indices, estimating asymptotic
intersection numbers, and constructing the crucial trivializations needed to prove the
main results. Section 3.2 presents the Hamiltonians we use for the acceleration data for
balls in CP n, and discusses the generators of their Floer–Morse–Bott complexes, which
are collected into three groups.
Section 4 deals with computing the indices of the generators mentioned in the previ-
ous paragraph. In Section 4.1, we discuss some useful properties of the Robbin–Salamon
index for paths of symplectic matrices. Then, in Section 4.2, we use those properties
alongside the three charts discussed previously to compute the Robbin–Salamon index
of those generators. Finally, in Section 4.3, we compute the Floer–Morse–Bott indices,
which adapt the Robbin–Salamon index to the Morse–Bott with cascades setting.
Sections 5 and 6 contain the Floer-theoretic heart of this paper. In Section 5.1,
we survey the connection between the intersection number of divisors with cylinders
and the relative first Chern number of the cylinder with respect to trivializations that
satisfy certain conditions related to the divisors themselves. We then prove that the
trivializations considered in Section 3 satisfy those conditions. Section 5.2 investigates
the intersection numbers of flowlines with cascades and divisors, including a discussion
on positivity of intersections and Seidel’s estimate for the asymptotic intersection num-
ber. Combined with the index formula, these results allow us to prove, in Section 5.3,
the emptiness of several moduli spaces of Floer, continuation, and homotopy flowlines
with cascades.
Next, Section 6 is dedicated to computing the Floer differential and continuation
maps associated with our acceleration data. It begins with a discussion of the no-escape
lemma (Section 6.1). In Section 6.2, we use this lemma to show that we can recover
an argument for computing the Floer complex in Cn and apply it to our acceleration
data, thus determining the algebraic counting for certain moduli spaces of Floer solu-
tions. In Section 6.4, we apply the algebraic results from Section 6.3 to carry out the
computations targeted in Section 6.
Section 7 constitutes the algebraic preparation required to prove the paper’s main
results.
First, in Section 7.1, we discuss modules over the Novikov ring and their
completions. We then deal with Schauder bases for completed modules and use them
31


## Page 32


to compute quotients; these will be utilized later when determining the homology at the
end of the relative symplectic cohomology computations for balls and their complements.
Later, in Section 7.2, we introduce the concept of a telescope—a model of the homotopy
colimit of a sequence of chain complexes upon which the definition of relative symplectic
cohomology relies. This involves higher homotopies between the Hamiltonians in two
different acceleration data sets. In contrast to the relative symplectic cohomology itself,
which can be computed without these homotopies, the restriction maps are usually
highly dependent on them. We prove that in our specific case, even though we do not
know how to explicitly compute those homotopies, we are still able to compute the
restriction maps using an indirect argument.
Section 8 provides a brief overview of the original definition of relative symplectic
cohomology based on classical Floer theory (Section 8.1). It then covers the definition
of, and equivalence to, a version of relative symplectic cohomology defined using Floer
theory in the Morse–Bott with cascades setting.
Section 9 combines the results from Sections 6, 7, and 8 to prove the main results of
the paper. Sections 9.1 and 9.2 handle the computation of topological energies and the
direct limit of the acceleration data chosen for the ball, presenting it in the form of the
Novikov modules discussed in Section 7.1. In Section 9.3, this allows us to compute the
relative symplectic cohomology of balls in CP n, thereby proving Theorem 1.1. Following
this, in Sections 9.4 and 9.5, we use these ideas together with Proposition 7.23 from
Section 7.2 to compute the restriction maps between balls, as well as from CP n to a
ball.
Section 10 is the shortest section of the paper. Here, we prove Theorem 1.11, which
asserts that the (algebraic) capacity of the relative symplectic cohomology is a lower
bound for the stable displacement energy.
The appendix consists of three sections. Appendix A contains the definitions of
Floer and continuation flowlines with cascades, as well as their moduli spaces, includ-
ing compactifications (Section A.1). We use these to provide upper and lower bounds
for the energy of those flowlines (Section A.2). We then combine this with the com-
pactness results to achieve transversality for data sufficiently close to our ideal data
from Section 3.2, while preserving the emptiness of the relevant moduli spaces (see
Sections A.3 and A.4).
In Appendix B, we prove Proposition 5.9, which is a slight generalization of Seidel’s
estimate for the asymptotic intersection number of a Floer cylinder with a divisor (see
[39, Eq. (7.22)]).
Finally, in Appendix C, we compute the relative symplectic cohomology of the com-
plement of toric balls in CP n and compute several restriction maps. Because the proof
is very similar to the case of the ball, we chose to include it in the appendix.

2
Morse–Bott approach to Floer theory
2.1
Basic definitions and conventions
A symplectic manifold (M, ω) is a pair consisting of a manifold M and a closed
2-form ω that is non-degenerate, meaning that for every point x ∈M, if there exists a
vector X ∈TxM such that ωx(X, Y ) = 0 for all Y ∈TxM, then necessarily X = 0.
An almost complex structure J on M is an automorphism of TM satisfying
J2 = −Id. An almost complex structure J is called ω-compatible if ω is J-invariant
and the pairing
⟨·, ·⟩ω,J = ω(·, J·)
defines a Riemannian metric on M.
A Hamiltonian H on (M, ω) is a smooth function H : S1 × M →R, where we
identify S1 with R/Z. If H depends only on M, it is called an autonomous or time-
independent Hamiltonian; otherwise, it is called a time-dependent Hamiltonian.
The symplectic gradient XH of H = (Ht : M →R)t∈S1 is the unique (possibly
time-dependent) vector field on M satisfying
ω(XHt, ·) = dHt,
33


## Page 34


where the differential d is taken with respect to the manifold M. Note that in our
conventions we have
XHt = −J∇Ht,
where the gradient is computed with respect to the Riemannian metric ⟨·, ·⟩ω,J.
The flow of XH is called the Hamiltonian flow of H, denoted by φt
H : M →
M, where t ∈R and φ0

The 1-time map φ1

diffeomorphism associated with H. Moreover, for every t ∈R, the diffeomorphism
φt

A 1-periodic orbit of H is a smooth map x: S1 →M satisfying
x(t) = φt

for every t ∈S1.

along x. Thus, given an ω-compatible almost complex structure J, we can define a

gJ,x(η, θ) =

2.1.2
Classical Floer theory
Throughout the rest of Section 2, let (M, ω) be a fixed closed monotone symplectic
manifold.
Let H ∈C∞(S1 × M) be a Hamiltonian. A 1-periodic x: S1 →M of H is called
non-degenerate if 1 is not an eigenvalue of the linearization of the return map, i.e.
dx(0)φ1
H : Tx(0)M →Tx(0)M. The Hamiltonian H is called non-degenerate if all of its 1-
periodic orbits are non-degenerate. Since M is closed, any non-degenerate Hamiltonian
has a finite collection of 1-periodic orbits.

2.2
Morse–Bott setting
Let H be an autonomous Hamiltonian on (M, ω). By the existence and uniqueness
theorem for ODEs, the Hamiltonian flow forms a one-parameter subgroup of diffeomor-
phisms and preserves the level sets of H. Moreover, if x: S1 →M is a 1-periodic orbit
of an autonomous Hamiltonian H, then for every t0 ∈S1, the loop xt0 : S1 →M given
by
xt0(t) = x(t −t0),
for every t ∈S1,
is also a 1-periodic orbit of H. Thus, every 1-periodic orbit of an autonomous Hamil-
tonian gives rise to an S1-family of 1-periodic orbits. Such S1-families are examples of
critical submanifolds of the action functional. In this section we introduce a Morse–Bott
theoretical approach to Floer theory that helps to overcome the fact that the critical
points of the action functional are not isolated.
2.2.1
Morse–Bott functions
Let (X, g) be a Riemannian manifold, possibly infinite-dimensional. Let A: X →R
be a C2-smooth functional. Then the gradient ∇A ∈Γ(X, TX) and the Hessian ∇2A ∈
Γ(X, TX ⊗T ∗X) of A are well-defined.

Trivializations and indices
Let H : S1 × M →R be a Hamiltonian, and assume that H satisfies the MB
condition. Let S ⊂L0(M) be a critical submanifold with respect to H.
Definition 2.9. Let τ = (τx)x∈S be a family of symplectic trivializations of TM along
the elements of S, i.e. for every x ∈S, τx is a symplectic trivialization of x∗TM. We
say that τ is a compatible symplectic trivialization along S if for every x, y ∈S
there is a path γ : [0, 1] →S such that the symplectic trivialization γ∗τy is homotopic
to the trivialization τx.4 In this case, we say that τ is obtained from cappings if
there exists x ∈S and a capping ˆx of x such that τx is homotopic to the trivialization
of x∗TM that is induced from ˆx.

Differential and continuation maps
In this section we discuss Floer differential and continuation maps by the Morse–Bott

Let H be a Hamiltonian satisfying the MB condition, and let J be an almost complex

2.6
Cubes
This section deals with algebraic, Hamiltonian and Floer theoretic cubes. We begin
with algebraic cubes:
46


## Page 47


2.6.1
Algebraic cubes
The language of cubes is very convenient in order to work with the algebraic data
arising when defining relative symplectic cohomology. Throughout we fix a unital com-
mutative ring R and we work in the category of graded R-modules and graded maps,
where the grading is over Z or over Zk with k even. Recall that given graded modules
C, D with graded components Ci, Di, a module map f : C →D is graded of degree
d if f(Ci) ⊂Di+d. We denote the degree of f by |f|.
Fix a nonnegative integer n. We call a subset of [0, 1]n a face if it is given by setting
some of the coordinates to either 0 or 1; the rest of the coordinates are referred to as the
free coordinates of F. Given a face F its dimension, denoted |F|, is the number of
its free coordinates, while its initial vertex ini F and terminal vertex ter F, are the
points of F closest to or farthest from the origin, respectively (relative to the Euclidean
metric); in this case we write F : v →v′, where v = ini F, v′ = ter F. Note that F is
determined by its initial and terminal vertices. We also say that v, v′ span F. If F ′, F ′′
are faces, we write F = F ′ · F ′′ to denote the situation in which
ini F = ini F ′ ,
ter F ′ = ini F ′′ ,
ter F ′′ = ter F .
An (algebraic) n-cube C is a pair ({Cv}v∈{0,1}n, {f C
F}F⊂[0,1]n a face), where each Cv
is a graded module, and for every face F ⊂[0, 1]n we have a graded module morphism
f C
F : Cini F →Cter F of degree 1 −|F|, subject to the condition that for each face F we
have
X
F=F ′·F ′′

where sgn(F ′, F ′′) is a sign defined as follows. Any face of [0, 1]n comes equipped with a
natural orientation coming from the ordering of its free coordinates. Then sgn(F ′, F ′′)
is the intersection index of F ′, F ′′ inside F. For a more explicit description, assume
first that we have a linearly ordered finite set S = {s1 < · · · < sk+l}. Recall that
a (k, l)-shuffle on S is a permutation σ ∈SS such that σ(s1) < · · · < σ(sk) and
σ(sk+1) < · · · < σ(sk+l). If S = S′ ⊔S′′ with |S′| = k, |S′′| = l, then there is a unique
(k, l)-shuffle σS′,S′′ such that σ({s1, . . . , sk}) = S′, σ({sk+1, . . . , sk+l}) = S′′. Now let

and S′′ the set of free coordinates of F ′′, so that S = S′ ⊔S′′. Endow S with the order
induced from {1, . . . , n}. Then sgn(F ′, F ′′) := sgn σS′,S′′.

2.6.2
Hamiltonian and Floer theoretic cubes
Fix a natural number n ∈N. Define g: [0, 1]n →R by g(x1, . . . , xn) = Pn
i=1 cos(πxi)
for every (x1, . . . , xn) ∈[0, 1]n. This is a Morse function whose critical points are the
vertices of the cube. Together with the Euclidean metric it forms a Morse-Smale pair,
and its gradient flow preserves the cube.
A monotone cube of Hamiltonians is a suitably smooth map H: [0, 1]n →
C∞(M), which is monotone nondecreasing along the gradient lines of g.
Similarly
to Varolgüneş’ definition, [44, 45], we assume H is constant on a neighborhood of each
vertex. Moreover, since we work in the Floer–Morse–Bott setting, we assume that the
Hamiltonians at the vertices satisfy the MB condition, in this case we say that H
satisfies the MB condition.
A cube of compatible almost complex structures is a suitably smooth map
J : [0, 1]n →J (M, ω). we assume J is constant on a neighborhood of each vertex.
In what follows, we describe Floer theoretic cubes associated to a generic choice of a
monotone cube of Hamiltonians H and a cube of compatible almost complex structures
J . For every vertex v ∈[0, 1]n, choose a Morse function hv on SH(v), and denote by
Cv the Floer complex CF ∗(H(v)) with the differential given by counting Floer flowlines
with cascades, as in Section 2.4.
For a face F ⊂[0, 1]n, denote v = ini(F) and v′ = ter(F).

follows:
Hγ = H ◦γ,

Recall that the face maps that correspond to each vertex are defined to be the Floer
differential. Also, note that by unraveling the definition, the face maps that correspond
to an edge are the continuation maps.
The cube relations follow from compactifying the moduli spaces
c
M(q−, q+; F) by
broken flowlines corresponding to the possible decompositions of the face F. Transver-
sality guarantees that no bubbling occurs in such broken configurations via the classi-
cal codimension argument. In a monotone symplectic manifold, sphere bubbling is a
codimension-2 phenomenon: removing the sphere bubbles from a bubbled broken con-
figuration yields an element of a moduli space whose dimension is at least two less than
that of the original configuration. Consequently, such bubbled configurations cannot
appear in the relevant 1-dimensional moduli spaces.
To achieve transversality, hence regularity of the moduli spaces involved, we use
a generic perturbation of the cube of almost complex structures, supported near the
vertices, and a generic perturbation of the cube of Hamiltonians, supported away from
the vertices.

B(1) admits a Hamiltonian S1-action, given by θ · z = (eiθz1, . . . , eiθzn) for every

We denote by µ: CP n →R its moment map,
normalized such that it satisfies
π∥z∥2 = µ ◦ρ(z),
for every z ∈B(1). Consequently, the image of µ is the interval [0, 1]. Note that µ is
invariant under the action of U(n) on CP n, induced from the action of U(n) on Cn via
the symplectic cut.

H = f ◦µ: CP n →R

3.1
Three charts
As mentioned in the introduction, our strategy for proving the nonexistence of some
Floer and continuation flowlines relies on estimates of intersection numbers with divi-
sors, combined with the index formula (see Remark 2.13). A crucial input for it, is
a choice of trivialization and the computation of the Robbin–Salamon indices of the
1-periodic orbits with respect to the chosen trivialization. In our setting, we consider
three different coordinate charts, each over a different subset of CP n. In this section,
we describe these charts, trivializations arising from them, and discuss the geometric
properties of the associated subsets.

3.1.2
The complement of the origin
In this section we construct a biholomorphism between CP n \{0}, where 0 = [1 : 0 :
· · · : 0] is the image of the origin in B(1) under ρ, and the hyperplane line bundle O(1)
over CP n−1. Recall that the hyperplane line bundle p: O(1) →CP n−1 is defined by
O(1) = {(w, λ) | w ∈CP n−1, λ ∈Hom(w, C)}.
Here, w is a 1-dimensional subspace of Cn, and λ is a linear functional on that line.
For every (w, λ) ∈O(1) and z ∈w \ {0}, the vector (λ(z), z) ∈C × Cn = Cn+1
is nonzero and hence determines a point in CP n. Define a map Φ: O(1) →CP n as
follows. For (w, λ) ∈O(1), choose z ∈w \ {0} ⊂Cn and set
Φ(w, λ) = [λ(z) : z].
The map Φ is well-defined. Indeed, let z, z′ ∈w \ {0}. Since w is a complex line, there
exists c ∈C \ {0} such that z′ = cz. Then
[λ(z′) : z′] = [λ(cz) : cz] = [cλ(z) : cz] = [λ(z) : z],
showing that Φ(w, λ) is independent of the choice of z.
Claim 3.5. The map Φ: O(1) →CP n \ {0} is a biholomorphism with respect to the
standard complex structures of O(1) and CP n \ {0} ⊂CP n.
Proof. Let us check all the required properties:
(1) Injectivity. Let (w, λ), (w′, λ′) ∈O(1) and suppose Φ(w, λ) = Φ(w′, λ′). Choos-
ing representatives z ∈w \ {0} and z′ ∈w′ \ {0}, the equality in CP n implies there
exists a scalar c ∈C \ {0} such that
(λ′(z′), z′) = c(λ(z), z) ∈C × Cn.
Comparing the vector components yields z′ = cz, which implies w′ = w. Comparing
the scalar components yields λ′(z′) = cλ(z). Substituting z′ = cz into the left side gives
λ′(z′) = λ′(cz) = cλ′(z).
Equating this with cλ(z) implies λ′(z) = λ(z). Note that w = w′ is a 1-dimensional
linear space and z is a nonzero vector, hence it is a basis for w. Since the functionals
agree on a basis, we conclude that λ′ = λ. Thus, (w, λ) = (w′, λ′). This shows that Φ
is injective.
58


## Page 59


(2) Surjectivity onto CP n \ {0}. Let [u0 : u] ∈CP n where u0 ∈C, u ∈Cn and
(u0, u) ∈C × Cn = Cn+1 is not the zero vector.
Note that the excluded point 0
corresponds to the case u = 0. For any u ∈Cn \ {0}, let w = span{u} ∈CP n−1.
Let λ ∈Hom(w, C) be the unique linear functional satisfying λ(u) = u0. Consider
(w, λ) ∈O(1). Then
Φ(w, λ) = [λ(u) : u] = [u0 : u].
Thus, the image is exactly CP n \ {0}.
(3) Holomorphicity. We work in standard local charts U0, . . . , Un−1 ⊂CP n−1 given
by
Uj = {[z0 : · · · : zn−1] ∈CP n−1 : (z0, . . . , zn−1) ∈Cn, zj ̸= 0},
for every j ∈{0, . . . , n −1}.
We will prove that for every j ∈{0, . . . , n −1} the restriction Φ|p−1(Uj) : p−1(Uj) →
CP n is holomorphic. For simplicity of notation, we will focus on the case where j = n−1.
All the other cases are similar. There exists a unique biholomorphism y: Un−1 →Cn−1
satisfying w = span{(y(w), 1)} ⊂Cn for every w ∈Un−15. Note that for every w ∈Un−1
the vector (y(w), 1) is a basis for w.
Thus, for every c ∈C there exists a unique
functional λc ∈Hom(w, C) satisfying λc(y(w), 1) = c. Let φ: Un−1 × C →p−1(Un−1)
be defined by φ(w, c) = (w, λc) for every (w, c) ∈Un−1 × C. Thus φ is a holomorphic
local trivialization of O(1) over Un−1. Now, note that
Φ ◦φ(w, c) = Φ(w, λc) = [λc(y(w), 1) : y(w) : 1] = [c : y(w) : 1] ∈CP n,
for every (w, c) ∈Un−1 × C.
Since the coordinates here are holomorphic, we de-
duce that Φ|p−1(Un−1) is holomorphic. By a similar proof, one can show that Φ|p−1(Uj)
is holomorphic for every j ∈{0, . . . , n −1}.
Since O(1) is covered by the charts
p−1(U0), . . . , p−1(Un−1), we deduce that Φ is holomorphic.
(4) Smoothness of the inverse map. We have already established that Φ is a holo-
morphic bijection onto CP n \ {0}. Therefore, according to [21, Page 19] we deduce
that Φ−1 is holomorphic as well. Thus we conclude that Φ is a biholomorphism, as
required.
Now we endow O(1) with a specific symplectic form which we will denote by Ω. Let
us construct this form. Let h be the Hermitian metric on the fibers of O(1), induced
by restricting to each fiber the standard Hermitian metric on Cn. Explicitly, for a pair

well as the homotopies between acceleration data corresponding to two balls of different
capacities, by varying ∆.) This will be crucial for constructing the restriction maps used
in the proof of Theorem 1.1 and Theorem 1.7.
Remark 3.11. Note that H0 is a non-positive C2-small Morse–Bott function.
Let us describe the generators of the Floer–Morse–Bott complexes for this accel-
eration datum. Given ∆∈(0, 1) and ℓ≥0, the Hamiltonian Hℓhas ℓ+ 2 critical
submanifolds, which come in three types of families.

(i) The global minimum at 0,
(ii) ℓspheres, S2n−1, consisting of the points where the slope is integral,
(iii) and the global maxima, forming a copy of CP n−1 at the divisor at infinity.
By choosing a perfect Morse function on each of these critical submanifolds we get the
following generators: The first family contributes a single generator which we denote
by ˇxℓ
0; each sphere contributes a pair of generators corresponding to the minimum and

contributes n generators, which we denote by ˇxℓ

ℓ+n.
In Section 4 we compute the Floer–Morse–Bott indices of these generators with
respect to two different trivializations. Later in Section 5 and Section 6 we compute
the differential of this complex and continuation maps between such complexes.
4
Indices
This section is dedicated to computing the Robbin–Salamon and Floer–Morse–Bott
indices of the 1-periodic orbits for our acceleration data from Section 3.2. Section 4.1
continues the discussion of the Robbin–Salamon index for paths of symplectic matri-
ces initiated in Section 2.1.1, and lists its basic properties. Section 4.2 focuses on the
Robbin–Salamon index of Hamiltonian orbits and provides its computation in several
important cases for the Hamiltonians used to compute the relative symplectic cohomol-
ogy of balls in CP n, defined in Equation (15). Finally, in Section 4.3, we compute the
Floer–Morse–Bott indices of these 1-periodic orbits.
4.1
Properties of the Robbin–Salamon index
In this section, we continue the discussion from Section 2.1.1 regarding the Robbin-
Salamon index for paths of symplectic matrices (see Definition 2.2).
Remark 4.1. The Robbin–Salamon index satisfies several further properties that will
be useful below.
• (Naturality) For every pair of paths Φ, Ψ: [0, 1] →Sp(2n), we have µRS(ΦΨΦ−1) =
µRS(Ψ);
• (Product) Given two paths Ψ1 : [0, 1] →Sp(2n1) and Ψ2 : [0, 1] →Sp(2n2) we
have µRS(Ψ1 ⊕Ψ2) = µRS(Ψ1) + µRS(Ψ2);

Given a smooth path Ψ: [0, 1] →Sp(2n) of symplectic matrices, it is a straight-

51].
Definition 4.2. Let Ψ: [0, 1] →Sp(2n) be a smooth path of symplectic matrices.
• A point t ∈[0, 1] is called a crossing if det(Ψt −Id) = 0.
• For t ∈[0, 1], the crossing form Γ(Ψ, t) is defined as the quadratic form which is
the restriction of St to ker(Ψt −Id), where St is the symmetric matrix satisfying
˙Ψt = J0StΨt. Note that in ker(Ψt −Id), we have ˙Ψt = J0StΨt = J0St, and thus
Γ(Ψ, t)(v) = ⟨Stv, v⟩= ω(Stv, J0v) = ω(−J0 ˙Ψtv, J0v) = ω(v, ˙Ψtv) = ⟨v, −J0 ˙Ψtv⟩.
• A crossing t0 is called regular if the crossing form Γ(Ψ, t0) is nondegenerate, i.e.
0 is not an eigenvalue of Γ(Ψ, t0).
Proposition 4.3. Let Ψ: [0, 1] →Sp(2n) be a smooth path of symplectic matrices. If
Ψ has only regular crossings then its Robbin–Salamon index satisfies
µRS(Ψ) = 1

4.2
Robbin–Salamon index for Hamiltonian orbits
In this section we compute the Robbin–Salamon index of the 1-periodic orbits of the
Hamiltonians used in the computation of the relative symplectic cohomology of balls in
CP n, as defined in Equation (15). The computation will be carried out with respect to
three different choices of trivializations. These calculations will play an essential role in
the analysis of Floer differentials and continuation maps. Throughout this section we
fix ∆∈(0, 1) and ℓ∈Z≥0, define hℓ: [0, 1] →R by hℓ(r) = h(∆, ℓ, r) for every r ∈[0, 1]
and focus on the Hamiltonian Hℓon CP n given by Hℓ(z) = hℓ(µ(z)) = h(∆, ℓ, µ(z)),
for every z ∈CP n, as in Equation (15).

4.3
Computations of Floer–Morse–Bott indices
Recall that the Floer–Morse–Bott index of a critical point p of a Morse function h
on a critical submanifold S, with respect to an autonomous Hamiltonian H satisfying
that MB condition and a symplectic trivialization τ of TM along p, is given by
µτ

Remark 4.6. Note that with respect to trivializations induced by capping disks of
contractible 1-periodic orbits, the Floer–Morse–Bott index, gives a well-defined number
modulo 2N, independent of the trivialization, where N is the minimal first Chern
number of (M, ω). We denote this index by µFMB. The modulo 2N ambiguity arises
because changing the capping by attaching a sphere results in the index being shifted
by twice the value of the first Chern class evaluated on that sphere.
We now return to the Hamiltonians Hℓfor ℓ∈Z≥0 and ∆∈(0, 1). We go over 1-

chosen on the families of orbits for which we computed the Robbin–Salamon index, and
calculate the Floer–Morse–Bott index.

5
Obstructions
5.1
Relative first Chern number and intersection number
Relative first Chern number for complex bundles
Let Σ be a closed surface, with Γ ⊂Σ a finite set of points. An open neighborhood
U of Γ is called disk-like if its closure U has a finite number of connected components,
each of them is diffeomorphic to a smooth closed disk and contains a single point from
Γ. Let E →Σ \ Γ be a complex vector bundle, of complex rank n. Since U \ Γ has
a homotopy type of a finite collection of circles, E|U\Γ is trivial, hence det E|U\Γ :=
ΛnE|U\Γ is trivial. Let τ be some complex trivialization of det E|U\Γ := ΛnE|U\Γ, thus
τ : U \ Γ × C →det E|U\Γ is an isomorphism of complex vector bundles. The relative
first Chern number cτ
1(E) of E with respect to the trivialization τ is defined to be the
76


## Page 77


algebraic signed count of zeroes of a generic section of det E|Σ\Γ, extending the locally
constant section τ(·, 1) of E|U\Γ.
One can define an equivalence relation on the set of pairs (U, τ) of disk-like neigh-
borhoods of Γ and trivializations τ of E|U\Γ by defining (U, τ) ∼(U ′, τ ′) provided that
there exists a disk-like neighborhood V ⊆U ∩U ′ such that that τ|V \Γ is homotopic to
τ ′|V \Γ. The relative first Chern number cτ
1(E) only depends on the equivalence class of
the pair (U, τ).
Note that a trivialization of the complex vector bundle E induces a trivialization of
det E by the n-th exterior product map, where n is the rank of E. Therefore we can
define a relative first Chern class with respect to a trivialization of E itself.
Relative first Chern number for complex real bundles
Let E be a real vector bundle over a base B of rank 2n for some n ∈N. A complex
structure J on E is an automorphism of E satisfying J2
x = −id on the fiber Ex for
every x ∈B.
We call the pair (E, J) a complex real bundle, to distinguish it
from a complex bundle where the fibers are vector spaces over C. A complex real
trivialization of (E, J) is a trivialization τ : B × R2n →E satisfying τ∗J0 = J, where
J0 is the standard complex structure on R2n.
Next, we show how a rank 2n complex real bundle (E, J) induces a rank n complex
vector bundle EC. Consider the complexification EC := E⊗RC. This is a complex vector
bundle of rank 2n. Extending J linearly over C, J acts on EC, and satisfies J2 = −id.
Thus, its eigenvalues are i and −i, and EC splits as the sum of two eigenbundles
EC = E1,0 ⊕E0,1, where E1,0 = ker(J −i) is the eigenbundle for i, and E0,1 = ker(J +i)
is the eigenbundle for −i. We consider E1,0 as the complex bundle associated to the
complex real bundle (E, J).
A complex real trivialization of (E, J), given in terms of a J-adapted frame e1, . . . , en,
Je1, . . . , Jen, induces a complex trivialization of E1,0 by the frame 1
2(e1−iJe1), . . . , 1
2(en−
iJen). This is the projection of e1, . . . , en on E1,0 along E0,1 with respect to the decom-
position EC = E1,0 ⊕E0,1.
Thus, given a complex real trivialization of (E, J), the relative first Chern number
is defined with respect to det E1,0 with its induced trivialization.
Relative first Chern number for symplectic bundles
A unitary bundle (E, J, ω) is a triplet where E is a 2n rank bundle over a base B,
(E, J) is a complex real bundle, (E, ω) is a symplectic bundle and J is compatible with
ω, that is, ω(·, J·) is a Riemannian metric on E. A unitary trivialization of (E, J, ω)
is a trivialization τ : B × R2n →E of E satisfying τ ∗J = J0 and τ ∗ω = ω0.

Relative first Chern number and divisors
Let (M, ω) be a symplectic manifold. Given a smooth symplectic divisor D, we
denote by O(D) the complex line bundle associated to D. This complex line bundle is
defined up to isomorphism of complex line bundles as a line bundle over M, admitting
a section vanishing transversally precisely at D. In particular c1(O(D)) = PD(D). We
briefly recall a construction below:
Since D is a closed symplectic submanifold, there exists a compatible almost complex
structure J on M such that D is J-holomorphic. Let ND := TM|D/TD be the normal
bundle of D, the quotient construction equips it with an almost complex structure
˜J descending from J.
There exists a tubular neighborhood U of D, which can be
identified with a neighborhood of the zero section, V ⊂ND. Denote by Φ: U →V
the identification, and by π: ND →D the bundle projection. Consider the tautological
section of π∗ND whose base is ND and for every v = (p, q) ∈ND where π(v) = q, the
fiber (π∗ND)v of π∗ND over v equals the fiber (ND)q of ND over q. Now, consider the
tautological section s: V →π∗ND given by s(v) = v ∈(ND)π(v) for every v ∈V . This
section vanishes transversely on D and away from D it is nonzero. Finally, consider the
pullback line bundle Φ∗(π∗ND) over U ⊂M, and its section Φ∗s. Since Φ∗s does not
vanish along U \D, it defines a trivialization of Φ∗(π∗ND)|U\D over U \D. Consider the
trivial line bundle (M \ D) × C →M \ D over M \ D and glue it with the line bundle
Φ∗(π∗ND) using the trivialization over U \ D, which is determined by Φ∗s, to obtain
a line bundle over M that coincides with Φ∗(π∗ND) over U and admits a section that
vanishes exactly along D and is transversal to D. The obtained line bundle is O(D).
Let m ≥1 and let D1, . . . , Dm be pairwise transversely intersecting smooth divisors.
Denote D = D1 ∪. . . ∪Dm. We define O(D) by O(D1) ⊗. . . ⊗O(Dm).
Assume that D is Poincaré dual to
1
N c1(TM), namely that c1(TM) = N · PD(D),
for some N ∈N. Since complex line bundles are classified up to bundle isomorphism
by their first Chern classes, there is a bundle isomorphism det TM ∼= O(D)⊗N. As seen
by their construction, the bundles O(Di) admit a section vanishing transversely on Di,
for every 1 ≤i ≤m; we call such a section a canonical section σDi : M →O(Di). The
tensor product σD = σD1 ⊗· · ·⊗σDm : M →O(D) is called a canonical section of O(D).
80


## Page 81


The identification det TM ∼= O(D)⊗N defines a section of det TM which we denote by
σN·D which is equal to σ⊗N
D , under this identification.
Note that the section σN·D vanishes to order N along D.
The section σN·D is an example of a D-canonical section: A section σ of det TM
is called canonical with respect to D, or simply D-canonical, if σ−1(0) = D and
there exists N ∈N such that for every x ∈D there exists a neighborhood Ux such that
σ = (g1 · . . . · gm)N · f · e where:
(i) e is a local section of det TM, nowhere vanishing in Ux;
(ii) f : Ux →C is a nonzero function;
(iii) for every 1 ≤i ≤m, the function gi : Ux →C has simple zeros along Di ∩Ux.

be a symplectic trivialization of det TM over V . Let u: Σ →M be a smooth map from
a Riemann surface with punctures Σ such that all the asymptotes of u are contained
in V . Then the relative first Chern number of u with respect to τ is denoted cτ
1(u)
and is defined to be cu∗τ
1
(u∗det TM|V ).
We now formulate and prove a relation between relative Chern numbers and inter-
section numbers, when the trivialization is taken to be via such canonical sections.
Lemma 5.3. Let (M, ω) be a closed symplectic manifold, let J be a compatible almost
complex structure and let D be a symplectic divisor (possibly a union of transversely
intersecting smooth divisors). Assume that
• There exists N ∈N such that c1(TM) = N · PD(D);
• There is a trivialization τ : (M \ D) × C →det TM of det TM along M \ D;
• There exists a D-canonical section s: M →det TM such that τ(·, 1) = s.
Let u: Σ →M be a smooth map from a Riemann surface with punctures Σ such that
all the asymptotes of u are contained in M \ D. Then the relative first Chern number
of u equals to N times the intersection number of u with D, i.e. cτ
1(u) = N(u · D).
Proof. Without loss of generality, we may assume that u is transverse to D. If D is
a union of smooth divisors intersecting transversely, then the intersection of any two
or more components has complex codimension at least 2. Hence, after a perturbation,
u may be assumed to intersect only the smooth locus of D.
This preserves cτ
1, as
transversality can be achieved by a homotopy of u supported near the intersection
locus, and cτ
1 is invariant under such homotopies.
81


## Page 82


The trivialization u∗τ of (u∗det TM)|u−1(M\D) is determined by the section σ =
u∗s, which is a section of u∗det TM. We may compute cτ
1(u) by a signed count with
multiplicities of the zeroes of this section σ.
Note that for every x ∈Σ, σ(x) = 0 if and only if u(x) ∈D. Since s vanishes
to order N along D, and since u is transversal to D, every zero of σ is of order N.
This shows that the relative first Chern number cτ
1(u) equals N times the intersection
number of u with D, i.e. cτ
1(u) = N(u · D), as required.

Proposition 5.6. Let Σ be a punctured Riemann surface and let u: Σ →M be a
smooth map, asymptotic at the punctures to loops. Assume that at the puncture p, the
map u is asymptotic to a constant loop. Let τ be a trivialization of u∗det TM over the
asymptotic loops such that at p the trivialization τ is induced by the constant capping.
Let ˜Σ be the surface obtained from Σ by filling in the puncture p and let ˜u: ˜Σ →M be
the continuous extension of u to ˜Σ. Then cτ
1(u) = cτ
1(˜u).
Proof. cτ
1(u) equals the signed count of zeros of a section of u∗det TM that is transversal
to the zero section and equal to a non-zero constant near the asymptotics with respect to
the trivialization τ. Since τ equals at p to the trivialization induced from the constant
capping, we can extend such a section to ˜Σ by requiring it to be constant over the
capping. This extension preserves the signed count of zeros because it introduces no
new zeros and remains transversal to the zero section. Consequently, this count equals
the signed count of zeros of a generic section of ˜u∗det TM, which is, by definition,
cτ
1(˜u).
5.2.1
Positivity of intersections
The first type of intersections with which we deal is intersections of a Floer solution
with a divisor. Let us start with a definition:
Let X be a finite dimensional smooth manifold and let Y ⊂X be a submanifold of
codimension 2. Let Σ be a Riemann surface and let f : Σ →X be a smooth map. Let
z ∈Σ and suppose that f(z) ∈Y is an isolated intersection of f with Y . This means
that there exists an open disk D0 ⊂Σ around z and a contractible open (in relative
topology) neighborhood U ⊂Y around f(z) such that f −1(U) ∩D0 = {z}. Then the
local intersection number
(f · Y )z := (f|D0) · B
is defined as the signed count of intersections obtained by a small perturbation of f
which makes it transversal to Y , and is independent of the choice of such perturbation.
More generally, suppose that Σ and Y are compact and connected with (possibly
empty) boundaries such that f −1(Y ) ∩∂Σ = f −1(∂Y ) ∩Σ = ∅.
Then we have a
well-defined intersection number f · Y of f with Y .
Now, the following result is known as positivity of intersections for Floer solu-
tions and symplectic divisors, it appears in the literature in several versions, here we
provide a variation on the formulation due to Ganatra–Pomerleano:
86


## Page 87


Proposition 5.7 ([20, Lemma 4.3]). Let (M, ω) be a symplectic manifold and let D ⊂
M be a smooth divisor.
Let J be a compatible almost complex structure on (M, ω)
and assume that D is a complex hypersurface with respect to J. Let β be a 1-form
on a connected Riemann surface (Σ, j), and let H : Σ × M →R be a Σ-parametrized
Hamiltonian on M satisfying XH(z, x) ∈TxD for every z ∈Σ and x ∈D. Let u: Σ →
M be a Floer solution for H and β, i.e., a solution of the equation
(du −XH ⊗β)0,1 = 0.
(16)
If D is connected and there exists an open subset V ⊂Σ with compact closure such that
u(Σ \ V ) ∩D = ∅, then u−1(D) is a finite set, and for each z ∈im u ∩D, the local
intersection number (u · D)z is at least 1.
Remark 5.8. The positivity of intersections established in Proposition 5.7 applies to
both the cascades part and the bubbles part of a bubbled flowline with cascades. The
latter are holomorphic, satisfying the condition (dBα)0,1 = 0.
5.2.2
Asymptotic intersection number
The second type of intersections with which we deal, are intersections of the asymp-
tote of a Floer solution with a divisor.
Let Σ be a Riemann surface with a puncture p, and let X be a finite dimensional
smooth manifold containing a submanifold Y of codimension 2. Suppose u: Σ →X is a
smooth map that is asymptotic at p to a point y ∈Y , and assume that im(u) ∩Y = ∅.
The asymptotic intersection number of u with Y , denoted u ∗Y , is defined to be
the intersection number of the capped map u#c with Y at y, where c is the constant
capping at y. The orientation of c is taken to be positive if the puncture at y is negative,
and negative if the puncture is positive.
Let R ∈R and let us denote by S+(R), S−(R) the half-infinite cylinders:
S+(R) = [R, +∞) × S1
and
S−(R) = (−∞, R] × S1.
Let (M 2n, ω) be a symplectic manifold and let J be a compatible almost complex
structure on (M, ω). Assume that (M, J) is biholomorphic to a product (W, J′)×(B, i)
where (W, J′) is a (2n−2)-dimensional almost complex manifold and B is an open disk
in C centered at the origin equipped with the standard complex structure.
Let H : M →R be a Hamiltonian and assume that its Hamiltonian vector field XH
has the form
XH(w, z) = V (w) + 2παiz,
where V is a smooth vector field on W and α ∈R \ Z is a non-integer real number.
The following result provides a slight generalization of a formula due to Seidel [39,
Eq. (7.22)].
87


## Page 88


Proposition 5.9. Let p ∈W be a zero of V , let S be a half-infinite cylinder and let
u: S →M be a Floer solution with respect to H and J that converges to (p, 0) and
satisfies im u ∩(W × {0}) = ∅.
• If S = S+(R), for some R ∈R, then u ∗(W × {0}) ≥−⌊α⌋.
• If S = S−(R), for some R ∈R, then u ∗(W × {0}) ≥1 + ⌊α⌋.
For completeness, and to ensure compatibility with our sign conventions, the proofs
of Proposition 5.7 and Proposition 5.9 are provided in Appendix B.

5.2.3
Internal asymptotic intersections
The notion of internal asymptotic intersection introduced here is particular for bro-
ken flowlines with cascades. Let H be an autonomous Hamiltonian on a closed symplec-
tic manifold (M, ω) satisfying the MB condition. A critical submanifold with respect
to H will be called mixed if it contains both constant and non-constant 1-periodic
orbits. We assume that H has no mixed critical submanifolds. We will identify criti-
cal submanifolds consisting of constant orbits with submanifolds of M by sending each
constant loop to its image.
Let q+ and q−be critical points of a Morse function defined on the critical subman-
ifolds of H. Suppose that u is a broken bubbled flowline with cascades connecting q−
to q+, with cascades u1, . . . , um : R × S1 →M.
Note that for every 1 ≤k ≤m −1, the asymptote of the cylinder uk at +∞is a
constant orbit if and only if the asymptote of cylinder uk+1 at −∞is a constant orbit.
Let 1 ≤k ≤m −1, and assume that the asymptote of cylinder uk at +∞is a
constant orbit belonging to a critical submanifold S with respect to H. In such case,

5.3
The obstructions
The purpose of this section is to prove nonexistence of some Floer, continuation and
homotopy solutions for the acceleration data (Hℓ)ℓ≥0 we defined in Section 3.2. We
use the formula for dimensions of moduli spaces, together with intersection formula for
relative first Chern number, computed in two trivializations.
We start with a technical claim, similar to Proposition 5.6, regarding relative and
non-relative first Chern class.
Claim 5.17. Let x, y: S1 →M be loops, and let ˆx, ˆy: ¯D2 →M be cappings for x and
y, respectively. Let τ be a trivialization of x∗det TM and y∗det TM induced by these
cappings. If u: R×S1 →M is a cylinder asymptotic to x at −∞and to y at +∞, then
cτ
1(u) = c1(ˆx#u#ˆy−1),
where ˆy−1 denotes the capping ˆy with its orientation reversed.
92


## Page 93


Proof. First, cτ
1(u) is defined as the signed count of zeros of a section of u∗det TM
that is transverse to the zero section and equals 1 near the asymptotes, considered in
the trivialization τ. Since τ is induced by the cappings, we can extend such a section
to the closed sphere ˆx#u#ˆy−1 by setting it to be constant and equal to 1 over the
cappings. This extension preserves the signed count of zeros because it introduces no
new zeros and remains transversal to the zero section. Consequently, this count equals
c1(ˆx#u#ˆy−1), by definition.
The following result is an immediate corollary of Claim 5.17 and will serve as a
convenient shortcut in the proofs of the subsequent results about obstructions on certain
flowlines with cascades.

J0-holomorphic sphere. In particular, ω(uj) ≥0 and by monotonicity c1(uj) ≥0. Since
all of our proofs are based on inequalities, we may omit these curves.

If j > ℓ+ n, then by (⋆) we get that (n + 1)r < 0 which means that r ≤−1 and
this is a contradiction. Thus j ≤ℓ+ n.
If j < ℓ+ 1, then j ≤ℓ, so 0 ≤ℓ−j. Since j + r ≥ℓ+ 1 we deduce that
r ≥ℓ−j + 1 > 0, thus by (⋆) we get that
(n + 1)(ℓ−j + 1) ≤(n + 1)r ≤ℓ+ n −j.
Since (n + 1)(ℓ−j + 1) = nℓ−nj + 1 + ℓ+ n −j we deduce that
n(ℓ−j) + 1 ≤0,
but
n(ℓ−j) + 1 ≥0 + 1 = 1,

Thus we get that j ≥ℓ+ 1. This shows that
ℓ+ 1 ≤i, j ≤ℓ+ n,
and we know that i = j + (n + 1)r, which implies that r = 0 and therefore i = j.

appearing both in u and v as u1, . . . , um and all the bubbles appearing in both as

asymptotes of u1, . . . , um, and do not contain the images of u1, . . . , um and {Bα}α∈A.

uj is J0-holomorphic, and extends to a J0-holomorphic sphere. In particular, ω(uj) ≥0
and by monotonicity c1(uj) ≥0. Since all of our proofs are based on inequalities, we
may omit these curves.

6
Computations of Floer complexes
The purpose of this section is to compute the Floer complexes with Z coefficients,
of the Hamiltoians forming the acceleration data we presented in Section 3.2, and the
continuation maps between them. For transversality of the Floer complexes we perturb
the almost complex structures, keeping them equal to Jstd in a shell surrounding B∆,
so that the no escape lemma remains valid. For the continuation maps we perturb the
homotopies presented in Section 3.2. All this can be done coherently and so that the
obstructions from Section 5 still hold. See Sections A.3 and A.4 for precise details.
Let ∆∈(0, 1). For every ℓ∈Z≥0, we will compute the Floer cochain complex of the
Hamiltonian Hℓ: CP n →R which is given by Hℓ(z) = h(∆, ℓ, µ(z)) for every z ∈CP n.
Fix ℓ∈Z≥0. For every 0 ≤i ≤ℓ, let r∆

for every 1 ≤i ≤ℓ, we define the (2n −1)-spheres
Sℓ,i = {z ∈CP n : h′
ℓ(µ(z)) = i},
and equip each with a perfect Morse function.
Furthermore, we equip the divisor
D∞= CP n−1 with a perfect Morse function.

Our approach for the proof of these results combines the obstructions on Floer
solutions and continuation maps we discussed in Section 5 with well known ideas from
the computation of the Floer complex of J-shaped Hamiltonians on Cn, such as those
[30, Section 3]. To apply these computations for our case in CP n, we first discuss in
Section 6.1 the no escape lemma, and in Section 6.2 we apply it and adapt the ideas from
[30, Section 3] to CP n to compute some matrix elements of the Floer differential. The
proof of Theorem 6.1 and Theorem 6.2 appear in Section 6.4, using algebraic properties
of the cochain complexes and continuation maps to deduce the missing matrix elements.
6.1
No escape lemma and Floer theory on open balls
In this section, we survey a construction of a robust Floer theory for Hamiltonians
on Int B(1) that are linear with respect to the Liouville coordinate in the complement
of a compact set and are equipped with a special almost complex structure. This is a
variation on the classical Floer theory in completed Liouville domains. The construction
is based on what is known as the “no escape lemma.” We present a version of this lemma
adapted from [34], which allows us to show that the algebraic count of Floer trajectories
between generators is well-defined, provided that these trajectories do not escape from
a certain compact subset. Consequently, this framework enables us to establish a well-
defined Floer theory for such Hamiltonians.
We start with the formulation of the no escape lemma. Let (V, ω) be an exact, not
necessarily compact, symplectic manifold with boundary ∂V , and let θ be a primitive
1-form for ω. We assume that ∂V is of negative contact type: the Liouville field Y ,
defined by ιY ω = θ, points strictly inwards. We denote the flow of Y by (φt
Y )t, which
is well-defined for sufficiently small t. In a sufficiently small neighborhood U of ∂V ,
we define the Liouville coordinate R such that ∂V = {z ∈V : R(z) = R0} for some
R0 ∈R. Specifically, for every z ∈U, let r(z) ∈R be the unique value such that

standard complex structure on Σ.

This shows that for every R0 ∈(0, 1), we can apply Lemma 6.3 to the exact sym-
plectic manifold VR0 = R−1([R0, 1)) = {z ∈Int B(1) : R0 ≤π∥z∥2 < 1}. This allows us
to define the Floer complex for admissible Hamiltonians on Int B(1).
Let H be a Hamiltonian on Int B(1) that is either nondegenerate or autonomous
and satisfies the MB condition. We say that H is admissible if there exist R0 ∈(0, 1),
m ∈R \ πZ and b ∈R such that for every z ∈Int B(1), R(z) ≥R0 implies H(z) =
mR(z) + b. In this case the constant m is called the slope of H. The assumption
m /∈πZ guarantees that all 1-periodic orbits of H are contained in
Int B(1) \ VR0 = R−1([0, R0)) = {z ∈Int B(1) : π∥z∥2 < R0}.
121


## Page 122


By Lemma 6.3, all Floer trajectories connecting the 1-periodic orbits of H must be
contained in
Int B(1) \ VR0 = R−1([0, R0]).
Therefore, by standard compactness techniques, the Floer complex CF(H; Z) of H is
well-defined. The homology of CF(H; Z) is called the Floer homology of H and it is
denoted by HF(H; Z).
Let H, H′ be admissible Hamiltonians on Int B(1), and assume that H ≤H′. A
homotopy (Hs)s∈R from H to H′ is called admissible if it satisfies the following condi-
tions:
(i) For every s ≤0, we have Hs = H, and for every s ≥1, we have Hs = H′.
(ii) ∂sHs ≥0.
(iii) There exists a non-decreasing functions m: R →R\{0}, b: R →R and a constant
R0 ∈(0, 1) such that for every s ∈R and z ∈Int B(1), the condition R(z) ≥R0
implies Hs(z) = m(s)R(z) + b(s).
Note that given two admissible Hamiltonians H, H′ on Int B(1), if H ≤H′ and
the slope of H is less than or equal to the slope of H′, then there exists an admissible
homotopy between them.
By Lemma 6.3, given two admissible Hamiltonians H, H′ with H ≤H′ and an ad-
missible homotopy between them, all continuation trajectories connecting the 1-periodic
orbits of H to those of H′ must be contained in a compact set. Therefore, the contin-
uation map CF(H; Z) →CF(H′; Z) is well-defined and independent of the choice of
admissible homotopy.
Additionally, given three admissible Hamiltonians H ≤H′ ≤H′′ such that the slope
of H is less than or equal to that of H′, which in turn is less than or equal to that of H′′,
the continuation map CF(H; Z) →CF(H′′; Z) is chain homotopic to the composition
of the continuation maps
CF(H; Z) →CF(H′; Z) →CF(H′′; Z),
hence they induce the same map on the homology level: HF(H; Z) →HF(H′′; Z).
Note that for every admissible Hamiltonian H and a constant C > 0, the Hamilto-
nian H + C is admissible and satisfies CF(H + C; Z) = CF(H; Z). Since H and H + C
have the same slope, there exists an admissible homotopy from H to H + C, and the
induced continuation map is the identity.
Let us formulate the following conclusion of the above discussion as a claim:
122


## Page 123


Claim 6.4. Let H, H′ be admissible Hamiltonians on Int B(1) with H ≤H′. If H
and H′ have the same slope, then the continuation map CF(H; Z) →CF(H′; Z) is a
quasi-isomorphism.
Proof. Since Int B(1) is bounded in Cn and H, H′ are admissible, there exists C > 0
such that H′ ≤H +C. Since there exist admissible homotopies from H to H′ and from
H′ to H + C, the identity map id: HF(H; Z) →HF(H + C; Z) = HF(H; Z) is equal
to the composition:
HF(H; Z) →HF(H′; Z) →HF(H + C; Z) = HF(H; Z).
This implies that the continuation map HF(H; Z) →HF(H′; Z) is an isomorphism;
in other words, the continuation map at the chain level CF(H; Z) →CF(H′; Z) is a
quasi-isomorphism, as required.

7.1.1
Completion of Λ≥0-modules
Completion of Λ≥0-modules plays an essential role in Varolgüneş’s definition of sym-
plectic cohomology; therefore, we dedicate this section to describing it and to recollec-
tion of important facts about it.
For every 0 ≤r ≤r′, we have Λ≥r′ ⊂Λ≥r, which provides a natural morphism
Λ[0,r′) = Λ≥0/Λ≥r′ →Λ≥0/Λ≥r = Λ[0,r).
Therefore, (Λ[0,r))r>0 is an inverse system.
Definition 7.1. Let A be a Novikov module. The completion of A, denoted by bA, is
defined as
bA = lim
←−
r
A ⊗Λ≥0 Λ≥0/Λ≥r,
where r runs over (0, +∞).
Remark 7.2.
• Completion is a functor b : Mod(Λ≥0) →Mod(Λ≥0).
• As a functor, the completion of an isomorphism is again an isomorphism.
• The completion functor automatically extends to a functor b : Ch(Λ≥0) →Ch(Λ≥0).
Specifically, if (C, d) is a cochain complex over Λ≥0, then the completion ( bC, bd)
is obtained by applying the completion functor to the underlying module and to
the map d: C →C. We also extend the completion functor to the categories of
graded Λ≥0-modules and graded cochain complexes by degree-wise completion.
• Given a Novikov module A, the maps A →A ⊗Λ≥0 Λ≥0/Λ≥r given by x 7→x ⊗1
for every r > 0 induce a natural map A →
bA. If this map is an isomorphism,
A is called complete. The interval modules are typical examples of complete
modules. On the other hand, Λ is not complete since bΛ = 0 (see [45, Page 599]).

Definition 7.3. Let A be a Novikov module and let (ai)i∈N be a sequence of elements
in A.
• Let a ∈A. The sequence (ai)i∈N converges to a if for every r > 0 there exists
N ∈N such that for every i > N we have ai −a ∈T rA.
• The sequence (ai)i∈N is a Cauchy sequence if for every r > 0 there exists N ∈N
such that for every i, j > N we have ai −aj ∈T rA.
By a standard argument, any convergent sequence is also a Cauchy sequence. Ad-
ditionally, as mentioned in [45, Section 2.3], the completion of a Novikov module is iso-
morphic to the module of equivalence classes of Cauchy sequences, where two Cauchy
sequences are equivalent if their difference converges to 0. Note that the completeness of
a module is equivalent to all Cauchy sequences being convergent. The following result
is well known; we include its proof for completeness.
Claim 7.4. Let A be a complete Novikov module and let (λi)i∈N be a sequence in A. If
limi→∞λi = 0, then the series P∞
i=1 λi converges.

8
Relative symplectic cohomology
This section is dedicated to relative symplectic cohomology. We survey its definition,
an analogous definition based on the theory of Floer–Morse–Bott with cascades, and
finally, we explain the equivalence between these definitions.
8.1
Definition of the relative symplectic cohomology
This section is dedicated to the definition of relative symplectic cohomology and
its associated restriction maps; for further details, see also [45] and [11, Section 4].
Throughout this section we fix a closed symplectic manifold (M, ω).
8.1.1

contractible 1-periodic orbits, is defined as

Remark 8.6. Varolgüneş proves in [45, Proposition 3.3.2] that both the relative sym-
plectic cohomology and its associated restriction maps are well-defined. Specifically,
any two acceleration data associated with a compact set define the same cohomology
up to a canonical isomorphism, and similarly for the restriction maps.
A practical way to compute relative symplectic cohomology in concrete examples is
to use the direct limit instead of a telescope. Indeed, given an acceleration datum H,
Lemma 7.21 asserts there exists a quasi-isomorphism:

8.2
Relative SH in Floer–Morse–Bott setting
The proof of Theorem 1.1 is based on a computation of the relative symplectic
cohomology in the setting of Morse–Bott with cascades (the Floer–Morse–Bott setting).
Let us briefly introduce this setting here. We also explain why the resulting relative
symplectic cohomology coincides with the original version surveyed in the previous
section. Let us start with the relevant modification of the Floer complex.
8.2.1
Weighted Floer–Morse–Bott complexes
As in Section 8.1.1, we define the weighted Floer complexes, but this time, in the
Floer–Morse–Bott setting.
Let H ∈C∞(S1 × M) be a Hamiltonian satisfying the MB condition, let h be a
Morse function on SH, let g be a Riemannian metric on SH, and let J be a compatible
almost complex structure on M.

namely

and sgn(u) is a sign determined by a system of coherent orientations chosen on the
moduli spaces. The exponent Etop(u) is the topological energy of the Floer flowline
with cascades u, see Equation (10) for the definition. Continuation maps are defined
similarly.
8.2.2
Relative symplectic cohomology
Given a monotone Hamiltonian cube H satisfying the MB condition and a cube of
compatible almost complex structures J , if the pair (H, J ) is regular, one can apply
the construction from Section 2.6.2 to obtain a Floer–Morse–Bott-theoretic cube, using
weighted Floer complexes, see Section 8.2.1. The vertices of this cube are the Floer
complexes of the vertices of (H, J ), and the face maps are defined by counting homotopy
flowlines with cascades of the corresponding local dimension. Following Definitions 8.1
and 8.2, acceleration data—both in general and for a compact set—must satisfy the MB
condition here, and Floer 1-rays are defined by considering weighted Floer–Morse–Bott
complexes.
Similarly to Varolgüneş [45, Section 3.3.2], given a compact subset K ⊂M and an
acceleration datum H = (Hi)i for K that satisfies the MB condition, the corresponding
Floer–Morse–Bott complex is defined as
SC∗
FMB(H) := c
tel CF(H).
Thus, the Floer–Morse–Bott relative symplectic cohomology of K inside M is
SH∗
M,FMB(K) := H

.
First, Varolgüneş’s filling result for partial monotone Hamiltonian cubes, [45, Propo-
sition 3.2.18] (see also [45, p. 558, 594]), directly translates to the Floer–Morse–Bott
165

setting. Consequently, since Floer–Morse–Bott theory with cascades extends classical
Floer theory, given two acceleration data H, H′ for a compact subset K such that H′
consists only of non-degenerate Hamiltonians, the proof of [45, Proposition 3.3.3] by
Varolgüneş is entirely adaptable. Thus, we conclude that SH∗

isomorphic to the original relative symplectic cohomology SH∗
