Let us remark that restriction maps are defined similarly and coincide with those
from the original definition.
9
Computations of relative symplectic cohomology
In this section, we apply our computations for Floer complexes from Section 6 to
compute the relative symplectic cohomology of toric balls in CP n and the restriction
maps between them, thereby proving Theorem 1.1 and Theorem 1.7.

9.2
Direct limit of Floer complexes
Throughout this section, we fix ∆∈(0, 1). As usual, for every ℓ∈Z≥0, define
hℓ: [0, 1] →R by hℓ(r) = h(∆, ℓ, r) for every r ∈[0, 1] and focus on the Hamiltonians
Hℓon CP n given by Hℓ(z) = hℓ(µ(z)) = h(∆, ℓ, µ(z)), for every z ∈CP n, as in
Equation (15).
The purpose of this section is to compute the direct limit of the 1-ray of the cochain
Floer complexes
CF(H0) →CF(H1) →CF(H2) →· · ·
where the connecting maps are the continuation maps. The description of the Floer
complexes and the continuation maps can be found in Theorem 9.1.

for every j ∈N and λ ∈Λ>j∆.
In this section, we compute the homology of the completion of (C, d). We start with
a new presentation of (C, d). Define a cochain complex ( ˜C, ˜d) by
˜C = Λ>0ˇx0 ⊕

j∈N
(Λ>0ˇxj ⊕Λ>0ˆxj) ,

for every j ∈N and λ ∈Λ>0. Now, define a map K : ˜C →C by
K = (id, T ∆id, T ∆id, T 2∆id, T 2∆id, T 3∆id, T 3∆id, . . . )
Claim 9.6. The map K is a cochain isomorphism.
Proof. The fact that K is an isomorphism of modules is straightforward. We check that
K is a cochain map. For every j ∈Z≥0 and λ ∈Λ>0, we have:
K ◦˜d(λˇxj) = K(0) = 0 = d(T j∆λˇxj) = d ◦K(λˇxj).
Additionally, for every j ∈N and λ ∈Λ>0, we have:
K ◦˜d(λˆxj) = K(T ∆λˇxj−1 + T n(1−∆)λˇxj+n)
= T (j−1)∆T ∆λˇxj−1 + T (j+n)∆T n(1−∆)λˇxj+n
= T j∆λˇxj−1 + T j∆T nλˇxj+n
= T j∆(λˇxj−1 + T nλˇxj+n)
= d(T j∆λˆxj)
= d ◦K(λˆxj).
Therefore, d ◦K = K ◦˜d, and we conclude that K is a cochain isomorphism.

10
Torsion and stable displacement energy
Given a subset X of a symplectic manifold (M, ω), we denote by ed(X) its displace-
ment energy. The stable displacement energy of X, denoted est(X), is defined
to be the displacement energy of the stabilization of X, that is, of X × S1 as a subset
of (M × T ∗S1, ω ⊕ω0), so that est(X) = ed(X × S1). Trivially, for every subset X we
have ed(X) ≤est(X).
Given a module A over the Novikov ring Λ≥0, its capacity is defined to be
c(A) = inf{r > 0 : T rA = 0}.
Note that c takes values in [0, +∞]. The module A is called torsion if c(A) < +∞.
In his thesis [44, Remark 4.2.8], Varolgüneş proved that given a closed symplectic
manifold (M, ω) and a compact subset K ⊂M, the displacement energy of K is bounded
from below by the capacity of SH∗
M(K; Λ≥0), that is,
c(SH∗
M(K; Λ≥0)) ≤ed(K).
In particular, if K is displaceable then SH∗
M(K; Λ≥0) is torsion, and if c(SH∗
M(K; Λ≥0)) =
+∞then K is non-displaceable7. The main result of this section is the following gen-
eralization of Varolgüneş’s result, denoted as Theorem 1.11 in the introduction:
Theorem 10.1. Let (M, ω) be a closed symplectic manifold and K ⊂M be a compact
subset. Then the stable displacement energy of K is bounded from below by the capacity
of SH∗
M(K; Λ≥0), that is, c(SH∗
M(K; Λ≥0)) ≤est(K).
Proof of Theorem 1.11. If K is stably non-displaceable, its stable displacement energy
is infinity, and the statement is trivially true. Assume that K is stably displaceable;
this implies that K × S1 is displaced by a Hamiltonian diffeomorphism of M × T ∗S1.
Since this diffeomorphism is Hamiltonian, we can replace it with a compactly supported
Hamiltonian diffeomorphism (φt)t∈[0,1] that displaces K × S1, that is, φ0 = id, φ1(K ×
7Due to [26], it is possible to prove that c(SH∗
M(K; Λ≥0)) = +∞if and only if K is a heavy subset
in the sense of [13]
197

S1) ∩(K × S1) = ∅and supp φ = S
t∈[0,1] supp φt is contained in a compact subset of
M × T ∗S1.
Thus, we can symplectically embed an open neighborhood of the support of φ into
M × T2, where T2 is the standard torus (S1 × S1, Adx ∧dy) with large enough area A,
such that the projection of K × S1 ⊂M × T2 to T2 = S1 × S1 has the form S1 × {0}
for some constant point 0 ∈S1 = R/Z.
We will prove that a copy of SH∗
M(K; Λ≥0) appears as a submodule of SHM×T2(K ×
S1; Λ≥0).
Thus, from Varolgüneş’s result [44, Remark 4.2.8] and the fact that the
capacity of modules over the Novikov ring is monotone with respect to inclusions, it
follows that
c(SHM(K; Λ≥0)) ≤c(SHM×T2(K × S1; Λ≥0)) ≤ed(K × S1) = est(K),
and this completes the proof.

## Page 200


A
Floer theory with cascades
A.1
Flowlines with cascades and moduli spaces
Throughout this section, let us fix some variables: Let (M, ω) be a fixed closed
symplectic manifold.
Let H, H+, H−be Hamiltonians over M, satisfying the MB
condition. Let SH, SH+, SH−denote the union of all the images of the covering map
p: f
L0(M) →L0(M) of all the critical submanifolds of AH, AH+, AH−, respectively.
Thus, each of SH, SH+, SH−is a finite disjoint union of smooth closed, finite-dimensional
manifolds of varying dimensions. Let h, h+, h−be Morse functions on SH, SH+, SH−,
respectively.
Let J, J+, J−be compatible almost complex structures, possibly time
dependent. Also, let g, g+, g−be Riemannian metrics on SH, SH+, SH−, respectively.
Moreover, let H = (Hs)s∈R be a homotopy of Hamiltonians and J = (Js)s∈R a homo-
topy of compatible almost complex structures such that Hs = H−and Js = J−for every
s ≤0, and Hs = H+ and Js = J+ for s ≥1.
Let g be a Riemannian metric on SH, chosen generically so that the Floer–Morse–
Bott package below is well-defined from the point of view of transversality.
Given two disjoint submanifolds S−, S+ ⊂L0(M), we denote by π2(S−, S+) the
collection of all homotopy classes of cylinders in M relative to S−and S+; that is,
homotopy classes of smooth maps u: R × S1 →M with a negative asymptote in S−
and a positive asymptote in S+.
Definition A.1. Let S−and S+ be two connected components of SH−and SH+, re-
spectively.
Let us denote by Mcont(S−, S+; H, J ) the collection of all continuation
solutions connecting a 1-periodic orbit in S−to a 1-periodic orbit in S+. Similarly, for
every A ∈π2(S−, S+), we denote by Mcont(S−, S+; H, J ; A) the moduli space of all
continuation solutions representing the homotopy class A.
Remark A.2.
• In the case where H−= H+ and H, J are constant homotopies,
the moduli space Mcont(S−, S+; H, J ) consists of Floer solutions, for this reason
we denote it by M(S−, S+; H, J), where J = J−= J+, possibly time-dependent,
and H = H−= H+. Additionally, M(S−, S+; H, J) admits a natural R-action.
• In cases where H and J are clear from the context, we use the shortened notation
M(S−, S+) = Mcont(S−, S+; H, J ), and for every A ∈π2(S−, S+), we write
MA,cont(S−, S+) = M(S−, S+; H, J ; A).
Proposition A.3. Let S−and S+ be critical submanifolds, components of SH−and
SH+ respectively, and let A ∈π2(S−, S+) be a homotopy class. Moreover, let τ−and
τ+ be families of compatible trivializations of det TM along the orbits of S−and S+,
respectively, and denote τ = τ+ ∪τ−.

Definition A.26. Let S−⊂SH−and S+ ⊂SH+ be corresponding critical subman-

A bubbled continuation solution connecting S−to S+ is a tuple u =
(u, (Tk)a
k=1, (pk)a
k=1) where u ∈Mcont(S−, S+) and either:
• a = 0 and the collections (Tk) and (pk) are empty; or

In the latter case, for every 1 ≤k ≤a, the rooted bubble tree is holomorphic with

s→−∞u(s, ·), and we denote this limit by lim

s→+∞u(s, ·) and denote this limit by lim
s→+∞u(s, ·).
The space of all bubbled continuation solutions connecting S−to S+ is denoted by
Mcont,bbl(S−, S+; H, J ).
If H−= H+, J−= J+, and the homotopies H, J are constant (i.e., Hs = H−
and Js = J−for all s ∈R), then any bubbled continuation solution is called a bub-
bled Floer solution and its underlying continuation solution is called the underlying
Floer solution.
In this case, the space of all bubbled Floer solutions connecting S−to S+ is denoted
by Mbbl(S−, S+; H, J), where H = H−and J = J−.
Remark A.27. As usual, when H and J are clear from the context, we abbreviate
these spaces to Mcont,bbl(S−, S+) and Mbbl(S−, S+), respectively.

If H−= H+, J−= J+, and the homotopies H, J are constant (i.e., Hs = H−and

bubbled Floer flowline.
Given a smooth manifold X, the natural action of R on itself induces an R-action on
the space of paths in X, denoted by C∞(R, X). For every s0 ∈R and γ ∈C∞(R, X),
the action of s0 on γ, denoted by s0 · γ, is defined by(s0 · γ)(s) = s0 · γ(s) = u(s + s0)
for all s ∈R. Similarly, the cylinder R × S1 admits a natural free R-action, given by
s0 · x = (s + s0, t) for every s0 ∈R and x = (s, t) ∈R × S1. That action naturally
induces an R-action on the space of cylinders in X, denoted by C∞(R × S1, X). For
every s0 ∈R and u ∈C∞(R×S1, X), the action of s0 on u, denoted by s0 ·u, is defined
by(s0 · u)(s, t) = s0 · u(s, t) = u(s + s0, t) for all (s, t) ∈R × S1.
Also, let us equip R × S1 with the standard flat Riemannian metric, obtained as a
pull back of the Euclidean metric on R2 under to quotient R × R →R × (R/Z). Given
a point x ∈R × S1 and a positive number ϵ > 0 we denote by B(x, ϵ) the open ball in
R × S1 of radius ϵ > 0, with respect to this metric, around a point x.

Theorem A.35. Let (ui)i∈N sequence of Floer (continuation) flowlines with m cascades.
If there exists a number E > 0 such that for all i ∈N, E(ui) < E, then (ui)i∈N has a

in the Morse–Bott Floer context.

Now consider the negative gradient flow segments γn

All the resulting broken pieces can be compartmentalized into a broken bubbled
flowline with cascades, where each piece begins at a negative gradient flowline of infinite
time, follows along cascades and finite time negative gradient segments, and ends at the
next gradient flowline of infinite time.
A.2
Energy bounds and bounds on the number of cascades
We show that the Floer and continuation flowlines that participate in the count for
the Floer differential operator, continuation morphisms and chain homotopies, all have
uniformly bounded energy and a uniformly bounded number of cascades, allowing us
later to apply compactness arguments for them. First we prove an energy bound. When
interpreting the following proposition, take N = 1 for flowlines in the chain differential,
N = 0 for flowlines in the chain continuation map and N = −1 for flowlines in the
chain homotopy.
Proposition A.40. Let (M, ω) be a closed monotone symplectic manifold. Let H−and
H+ be Hamiltonians on (M, ω) satisfying the MB condition, and let H = (Hs)s∈R be
a homotopy of Hamiltonians satisfying Hs = H−for every s ≤0, and Hs = H+ for

almost complex structures J−and J+ on M compatible with ω, possibly time-dependent,
for every homotopy of compatible almost complex structures J = (Js)s∈R, satisfying
Js = J−for every s ≤0 and Js = J+ for every s ≥1, for every critical points
q−∈Crit(h−) and q+ ∈Crit(h+), for every m ∈N, for every a continuation flowline u

Proof. Let N ∈Z. Since H−and H+ satisfy the MB condition and M is a closed
symplectic manifold, it follows that SH−and SH+ are compact. Therefore, the sets of
critical points Crit(h−) and Crit(h+) of h−and h+, respectively, are finite. For every 1-
periodic orbit α ∈SH−∪SH+, let us choose a capping ˆα, in a manner that is compatible
with index and action along every connected component of SH−∪SH+. This is done by
picking an arbitrary orbit at each connected component, choosing a capping for it, and
extending a capping to any other orbit along the component, but choosing paths from it
to any other orbit, lifting them to cylinders, and attaching them to the chosen capping.
Denote by τ0 the trivialization of det TM along all 1-periodic orbits corresponding to
critical points of either h−or h+, induced by the chosen cappings.
The constant E that will be considered in this proof is defined as the sum of three
constants. Each of these three constants is well-defined due to the fact that Crit(h−)
and Crit(h+) are finite sets. The constants are the following:

Next we prove a lower bound for the energy of every non-constant Floer solution.
Proposition A.41. Let (M, ω) be a closed monotone symplectic manifold. Let H be
an autonomous Hamiltonian satisfying the MB condition, and let h: SH →R be a
Morse function. Then there exists ℏ> 0 such that for every almost complex structure J
on M, any critical submanifolds S−, S+ ⊂SH, and every non-constant Floer solution
u ∈M(S−, S+; H, J) connecting S−to S+, the energy of u satisfies
Etop(u) ≥ℏ.
226

Proof. Denote by κ the monotonicity constant of (M, ω). For every 1-periodic orbit x
of H that is a critical point of h, choose a capping ˆx. Since SH is compact, Crit(h) is
finite. For this reason, the set

Consider ℏ= inf B. Since κZ is discrete and A is finite, it follows that B is discrete;
in particular, 0 is not an accumulation point of B. This implies that ℏ> 0.
Let J be an almost complex structure on M, let S−, S+ ⊂SH be critical submani-
folds, and let u ∈M(S−, S+; H, J) be a non-constant Floer solution connecting S−to
S+. Let us prove that Etop(u) ≥ℏ.
Since u is non-constant, Etop(u) > 0. Denote by q−∈S−and q+ ∈S+ the or-

Our construction of a direct system of Floer complexes, for the sake of computing
the relative symplectic cohomology of the ball, relies on constructing acceleration data
which is both regular and satisfies the vanishing properties of matrix elements described
in Theorems 5.19 and 5.21. In Proposition A.46 we construct it in stages, as a stabilizing
limit of finite sequences, where in each step we extend the sequence of length m by 1,
while possibly changing only the m’th step. hence there exists a stable limit.
Moreover, we make sure that all almost complex structures we construct remain
equal to Jstd on a shell A(r2, r1) := Br2 −Br1 for some fixed 1 > r2 > r1 > ∆. This is
necessary to be able to apply the no escape lemma (Lemma 6.3).
Regularity for continuation maps is achieved by a generic perturbation of the homo-
topy, whereas regularity for the Floer differential is achieved by perturbing Jstd in the
complement of A(r2, r1). Since all the Floer complex generators lie in the complement
of A(r2, r1), all Floer solutions intersect the support of the perturbation in an open
set, hence regularity is guaranteed. See [14, Chapter 2] for transversality results in
Morse-Bott with cascades, in the Rabinowitz Floer homology, [7, Proposition 3.5], for
transversality in Floer homology with cascades for autonomous Hamiltonians, and [18]
for transvesality in the nondegenerate case. many of the ideas carry to the Morse-Bott
case.

C.2
Floer–Morse–Bott indices
Recall that the Floer–Morse–Bott index of a critical point p of a Morse function h
on a critical submanifold S, with respect to an autonomous Hamiltonian H satisfying
that MB condition and a trivialization τ along p, is given by
µτ
FMB(p; H) = µτ
RS(p; H) + 1
2 dim M −1
2 dim S + indh p
where indh p is the Morse index of p as a critical point of h.
Remark C.2. Note that the Floer–Morse–Bott index is defined globally modulo 2N,
where N is the minimal first Chern number. We denote this index by µFMB. This
holds for every 1-periodic orbit of a Hamiltonian satisfying the MB condition, where
the index is computed using a trivialization obtained from cappings. The modulo 2N
ambiguity arises because changing the capping by attaching a sphere results in the index
being shifted by twice the first Chern class evaluated on that sphere.

C.2.1
Asymptotic intersection number
The second type of intersections with which we deal is not the intersections of Floer
solutions themselves with a divisor, but rather the intersections of their asymptotics.
Let Σ be a Riemann surface with a puncture p, and let X be a finite dimensional
smooth manifold containing a submanifold Y of codimension 2. Suppose u: Σ →X is a
smooth map that is asymptotic at p to a point y ∈Y , and assume that im(u) ∩Y = ∅.
The asymptotic intersection number of u with Y , denoted u ∗Y , is defined to be
the intersection number of the capped map u#c with Y at y, where c is the constant
capping map at y. The orientation of c is taken to be positive if the puncture at y is
negative, and negative if the puncture is positive.
Let R ∈R and let us denote by S+(R), S−(R) the half-infinite cylinders:
S+(R) = [R, +∞) × S1
and
S−(R) = (−∞, R] × S1.
Let (M 2n, ω) be a symplectic manifold and let J be a compatible almost complex
structure on (M, ω). Assume that (M, J) is biholomorphic to a product (W, J′)×(B, i)
where (W, J′) is an (n −1)-complex-dimensional complex manifold and B is an open
disk in C centered at the origin equipped with the standard complex structure.
Let H : M →R be a Hamiltonian and assume that its Hamiltonian vector field XH
has the form
XH(w, z) = V (w) + 2παiz,
where V is a smooth vector field on W and α ∈R \ Z is a non-integer real number.
The following result provides a slight generalization of a formula due to Seidel [39,
Eq. (7.22)].
246


## Page 247


Proposition C.3. Let p ∈W be a zero of V , let S be a half-infinite cylinder and let
u: S →M be a Floer solution with respect to H and J that converges to (p, 0) and
satisfies im u ∩(W × {0}) = ∅.
• If S = S+(R), for some R ∈R, then u ∗(W × {0}) ≥−⌊α⌋.
• If S = S−(R), for some R ∈R, then u ∗(W × {0}) ≥1 + ⌊α⌋.
For completeness, and to ensure the compatibility of our sign conventions, the proof
Proposition 5.9 is provided in Appendix B.
Let us present some examples that will be useful later:
Example C.4. Let ∆∈(0, 1) and ℓ∈Z≥0. Let S be a half-infinite cylinder and let
u: S →CP n be a Floer solution with respect to Hℓand the standard complex structure
J on CP n. Assume that im u ∩(D1 ∪· · · ∪Dn ∪D∞) = ∅.
• Assume that the asymptote of u lies on D∞= CP n−1. According to Claim 3.8
and the definition of the Hamiltonian Gℓ, we can identify a neighborhood of D∞⊂
CP n with a neighborhood U of the zero section of the line bundle O(1) →CP n−1.
In these coordinates, Gℓtakes the form
Gℓ(w, λ) =
−ϵ
1 + ∥λ∥2
h
,
for some ϵ ∈(0, 1), and the Hamiltonian vector field is given by
XGℓ(w, λ) = −2πiϵλ,
for every (w, λ) ∈U.
Since O(1) is a holomorphic bundle, there is a neighborhood of the asymptote of
u which is biholomorphic to a product W × B, where W is an open set in CP n−1
and B ⊂C is a small ball, both equipped with the standard complex structure.
Moreover, we can choose this biholomorphism such that XGℓtakes the form
XGℓ(w, z) = −2πiϵz,
for every (w, z) ∈W × B.
Thus, by Proposition 5.9 and the fact that im u ∩D∞= ∅, if u has an asymptote
at +∞on D∞, then
u · D∞= u · (W × {0}) ≥1.
Similarly, if u has an asymptote at −∞on D∞, then
u · D∞= u · (W × {0}) ≥0.

C.3
Obstructions
Theorems C.5 and C.7 below focus on the non-existence of certain Floer and contin-
uation flowlines with cascades associated with our acceleration data. The main tool in
their proofs is the positivity of intersection discussed in the previous section. To ensure
that the conditions for positivity of intersection hold, we should verify that for a given
broken bubbled flowline u with bubbles {Bα}α∈A and m cascades u1, . . . , um having
asymptotes qk
−and qk
+ at −∞and +∞respectively, any of these asymptotes that is
not constant does not intersect the divisors D1, . . . , Dn for every 1 ≤k ≤m. Given
Hamiltonians Gℓand G′
ℓ′ from our acceleration data associated with balls of capacity
∆and ∆′ respectively, for some ℓ, ℓ′ ∈Z≥0, we note that any element of the unitary
group U(n) induces a symplectomorphism of CP n that preserves the divisor D∞, the
Hamiltonians Gℓand G′
ℓ′, and all the Floer–Morse–Bott indices of their 1-periodic or-
bits. We thus deduce that there exists a unitary matrix U such that the transformed
divisors U(D1) ∪· · · ∪U(Dn) do not intersect any of the non-constant 1-periodic orbits
in the collection {q1
−, q1
+, . . . , qm
−, qm
+ }, and do not contain the images of u1, . . . , um or
{Bα}α∈A. Therefore, we can relabel U(D1), . . . , U(Dn) as D1, . . . , Dn, allowing us to
assume this configuration in the proof of each of these theorems.
Throughout this section and under the assumption above, we utilize two distinct
trivializations of the tangent bundle of CP n along the 1-periodic orbits of Gℓand G′
ℓ′,
denoted by τB and τT.

C.4
Computations of Floer complexes
The purpose of this section is to compute the Floer complexes, with coefficients in
Z, of the Hamiltonians from the acceleration data presented in Section C.1, and the
continuation maps between them.
Let ∆∈(0, 1). For every ℓ∈Z≥0, we will compute the Floer cochain complex of the
Hamiltonian Gℓ: CP n →R which is given by Gℓ(z) = g(∆, ℓ, µ(z)) for every z ∈CP n.

Sℓ,i = {z ∈CP n : g′
ℓ(µ(z)) = −i},
each equipped with a perfect Morse function. We also equip the divisor D∞= CP n−1
with a perfect Morse function.

C.5
Proof of Theorem C.8 and Theorem C.9
Let us start with the following auxiliary lemma:
Lemma C.11. For every ℓ∈Z≥0 and each j satisfying −(n −1) ≤j ≤ℓ+ 1, we have

does not intersect D∞otherwise.

C.7
Direct limit of Floer complexes
Throughout this section, we fix ∆∈(0, 1). As usual, for every ℓ∈Z≥0, define
gℓ: [0, 1] →R by gℓ(r) = g(∆, ℓ, r) for every r ∈[0, 1] and focus on the Hamiltonians
Gℓon CP n given by Gℓ(z) = gℓ(µ(z)) = g(∆, ℓ, µ(z)), for every z ∈CP n.
The purpose of this section is to compute the direct limit of the 1-ray of the cochain
Floer complexes
CF(G0) →CF(G1) →CF(G2) →· · ·
where the connecting maps are the continuation maps. The description of the Floer

Let us introduce a new presentation of (C, d). Define a cochain complex ( ˜C, ˜d) by
˜C = Λ>0ˇx−(n−1) ⊕· · · ⊕Λ>0ˇx0 ⊕
M

for every j ∈{1, . . . , n} and λ ∈Λ>0.
Now, define a map K : ˜C →C such that for every λ ∈Λ>0:
• K(λˇxj) = λˇxj for every −(n −1) ≤j ≤0;
• K(λˇxj) = T −j∆λˇxj for every j ∈N;
281


## Page 282


• K(λˆxj) = T −j∆λˆxj for every j ∈N.
Claim C.16. The map K is a cochain isomorphism.
Proof. It is straightforward to verify that K is an isomorphism of modules. We check
that K is a cochain map. For every j ∈Z≥−(n−1) and λ ∈Λ>0, we have:
K ◦˜d(λˇxj) = K(0) = 0 = d(K(λˇxj)).
Next, we verify the differential for the ˆxj generators. For every j ∈Z≥n+1 and
λ ∈Λ>0, we have:
K ◦˜d(λˆxj) = K(T ∆λˇxj+1 + T n(1−∆)λˇxj−n)
= T −(j+1)∆T ∆λˇxj+1 + T −(j−n)∆T n(1−∆)λˇxj−n
= T −j∆λˇxj+1 + T −j∆+n∆+n−n∆λˇxj−n
= T −j∆λˇxj+1 + T −j∆T nλˇxj−n
= T −j∆(λˇxj+1 + T nλˇxj−n)
= d(T −j∆λˆxj)
= d ◦K(λˆxj).
Finally, for every j ∈{1, . . . , n} and λ ∈Λ>0, note that j −n ≤0, so K acts as the
identity on the ˇxj−n terms. Thus, we have:
K ◦˜d(λˆxj) = K(T ∆λˇxj+1 + T j(1−∆)λˇxj−n)
= T −(j+1)∆T ∆λˇxj+1 + T j(1−∆)λˇxj−n
= T −j∆λˇxj+1 + T −j∆+jλˇxj−n
= T −j∆(λˇxj+1 + T jλˇxj−n)
= d(T −j∆λˆxj)
= d ◦K(λˆxj).
Therefore, d ◦K = K ◦˜d, and we conclude that K is a cochain isomorphism.
